哺乳动物 nectin-4 直系同源物对麻疹病毒的广泛许可:海豚 nectin-4 中的一个显着例外

Broad permissiveness of mammalian nectin-4 orthologs for morbilliviruses: a notable exception in dolphin nectin-4
作者： Mizuki Tominaga, Fumio Seki, Hiroki Nagata, Ayumu Hyodo, Yukiko Akahori, Yuki Kitai, Hiroshi Katoh, Makoto Takeda
摘要中文翻译麻疹病毒利用信号淋巴细胞激活分子 (SLAM) 和 nectin-4 作为其主要细胞受体,尽管共享共同的受体用途,但仍表现出独特的 宿主 特异性。虽然 SLAM 的物种特异性差异已得到广泛研究,但由于 nectin-4 在哺乳动物中高度保守,因此 nectin-4 对麻疹病毒 宿主 范围的贡献仍知之甚少。在这里,我们系统地比较了来自人类、海豚、蝙蝠、狗和海豹的nectin-4直系同源物对代表性麻疹病毒的受体活性,包括麻疹病毒(MV)、鲸类麻疹病毒(CeMV)、犬瘟热病毒(CDV)、海麻瘟热病毒(PDV)和鼠耳蝠麻疹病毒(MBaMV)。细胞-细胞融合、病毒感染和噬菌斑测定表明,大多数 nectin-4 直系同源物有效支持所有测试病毒的感染。相比之下,海豚nectin-4对MV、PDV和MBaMV的受体活性显着降低,同时对CeMV和CDV保持完全功能。这种表型在多种临床 MV 分离株中得到一致观察。突变分析确定氨基酸残基 63 是该受体特异性的关键决定因素。用甘氨酸取代海豚 nectin-4 中第 63 位的谷氨酸,使 MV 受体活性恢复到与人 nectin-4 相当的水平。这些发现表明,nectin-4 通常是一种广泛允许的麻疹病毒受体,但特定的氨基酸差异可以显着影响受体的使用。我们的结果表明,受体相容性有助于麻疹病毒 宿主 范围和跨物种 传播,但其本身不足以完全解释病毒 宿主 特异性。
查看研究七要素
问题与背景
麻疹病毒利用 SLAM 和 nectin-4 作为主要受体,但由于 nectin-4 在哺乳动物中的高度保守性,宿主 的特异性仍然知之甚少。本研究旨在探讨 nectin-4 在确定麻疹病毒 宿主 范围中的作用,重点关注 麻疹病毒。
设计与对象
该研究系统地比较了来自人类、海豚、蝙蝠、狗和海豹的nectin-4直系同源物对代表性麻疹病毒的受体活性,包括麻疹病毒、鲸类麻疹病毒、犬瘟热病毒、福辛瘟热病毒和鼠耳蝠麻疹病毒。
核心方法
使用细胞-细胞融合、病毒感染和噬菌斑测定来评估 nectin-4 直向同源物的受体活性。突变分析确定了影响受体使用的特定氨基酸残基。
主要结果
大多数 nectin-4 直系同源物有效支持所有测试的麻疹病毒(包括 麻疹病毒)的感染。Dolphin nectin-4 对 麻疹病毒、海狗瘟热病毒和鼠耳蝠麻疹病毒的受体活性显着降低,但对鲸豚麻疹病毒和犬瘟热病毒仍具有完全功能。
解释与创新
研究结果表明,nectin-4 通常是麻疹病毒广泛允许的受体,但特定氨基酸差异可显着影响受体的使用,突出了 宿主 系列和跨物种 传播 中受体兼容性的重要性。
科研与公卫意义
这些结果提供了对 麻疹病毒 宿主 特异性的分子机制的见解,并可能为了解病毒 传播 和制定有针对性的干预措施提供策略。
局限与证据强度
该研究仅基于摘要证据,虽然它确定了关键的氨基酸差异,但需要进一步的实验验证来确认这些发现的功能含义。由于分析范围有限,证据强度中等。

Broad permissiveness of mammalian nectin-4 orthologs for morbilliviruses: a notable exception in dolphin nectin-4

Authors: Mizuki Tominaga, Fumio Seki, Hiroki Nagata, Ayumu Hyodo, Yukiko Akahori, Yuki Kitai, Hiroshi Katoh, Makoto Takeda
Original abstract (English)
Morbilliviruses utilize signalling lymphocytic activation molecule (SLAM) and nectin-4 as their major cellular receptors and exhibit distinct host specificities despite sharing common receptor usage. While species-specific differences in SLAM have been extensively investigated, the contribution of nectin-4 to morbillivirus host range remains poorly understood because nectin-4 is highly conserved among mammals. Here, we systematically compared the receptor activities of nectin-4 orthologs from human, dolphin, bat, dog and seal for representative morbilliviruses, including measles virus (MV), cetacean morbillivirus (CeMV), canine distemper virus (CDV), phocine distemper virus (PDV) and Myotis bat morbillivirus (MBaMV). Cell–cell fusion, virus infection and plaque assays demonstrated that most nectin-4 orthologs efficiently supported infection by all tested viruses. In contrast, dolphin nectin-4 exhibited markedly reduced receptor activity for MV, PDV and MBaMV, while remaining fully functional for CeMV and CDV. This phenotype was consistently observed with multiple clinical MV isolates. Mutational analyses identified amino acid residue 63 as a key determinant of this receptor specificity. Substitution of glutamic acid at position 63 in dolphin nectin-4 with glycine restored MV receptor activity to a level comparable to that of human nectin-4. These findings demonstrate that nectin-4 is generally a broadly permissive morbillivirus receptor but that specific amino acid differences can substantially influence receptor usage. Our results indicate that receptor compatibility contributes to morbillivirus host range and cross-species transmission while being insufficient on its own to fully explain viral host specificity.
View research seven-element analysis
Question and background
Morbilliviruses utilize SLAM and nectin-4 as major receptors, yet host specificities remain poorly understood due to nectin-4's high conservation across mammals. This study aimed to investigate the role of nectin-4 in determining the host range of morbilliviruses, with a focus on measles virus.
Design and population
The study systematically compared the receptor activities of nectin-4 orthologs from human, dolphin, bat, dog, and seal for representative morbilliviruses, including measles virus, cetacean morbillivirus, canine distemper virus, phocine distemper virus, and Myotis bat morbillivirus.
Core methods
Cell–cell fusion, virus infection, and plaque assays were used to assess the receptor activities of nectin-4 orthologs. Mutational analyses identified specific amino acid residues that influenced receptor usage.
Main results
Most nectin-4 orthologs efficiently supported infection by all tested morbilliviruses, including measles virus. Dolphin nectin-4 showed markedly reduced receptor activity for measles virus, phocine distemper virus, and Myotis bat morbillivirus, but remained fully functional for cetacean morbillivirus and canine distemper virus.
Interpretation and novelty
The findings demonstrate that nectin-4 is generally a broadly permissive receptor for morbilliviruses, but specific amino acid differences can significantly affect receptor usage, highlighting the importance of receptor compatibility in host range and cross-species transmission.
Scientific and public-health significance
These results provide insights into the molecular mechanisms underlying measles virus host specificity and may inform strategies for understanding viral transmission and developing targeted interventions.
Limitations and evidence strength
The study is based on abstract-only evidence, and while it identifies key amino acid differences, further experimental validation is needed to confirm the functional implications of these findings. The evidence is moderate in strength due to the limited scope of the analysis.

患病率 以及乌干达 Kisoro 总医院 12-23 个月儿童未完成免疫接种的相关因素:一项横断面研究

Prevalence and factors associated with immunization non-completion among children aged 12–23 months at Kisoro General Hospital, Uganda: a cross-sectional study
作者： Grace Acomo, James Nelson Okema, Ronald Obal, Faith Akumu Foscah, Ikiriza Mary Lourdes, Martin John Sesay, Fonti Bangura, Joel Bulitya, John LR Elioba
摘要中文翻译
查看研究七要素
问题与背景
未完成免疫接种在此定义为儿童在乌干达国家免疫计划 (UNEPI) 中缺少至少一剂适合年龄的 疫苗 剂量,仍然是撒哈拉以南非洲地区的一个严重公共卫生问题,使儿童面临可预防疾病的风险。
设计与对象
2024 年 10 月至 2024 年 12 月,Kisoro 总医院对 275 名前往免疫诊所就诊的 12-23 个月儿童的护理人员进行了一项横断面研究,这些护理人员是通过系统随机抽样选出的。
核心方法
通过结构化的、预先测试的问卷和经过验证的免疫卡记录收集数据。使用 Stata 15.1 版(StataCorp,College Station,TX,USA)进行描述性统计、双变量卡方检验和多变量逻辑回归。
主要结果
The prevalence of immunization non-completion was 34.2% (94/275; 95% CI: 28.5–39.8%). In multivariable analysis, significant predictors included lack of formal caregiver education (aOR = 3.3, 95% CI: 0.93–11.49; p = 0.044), delayed initiation of immunization beyond three weeks of birth (aOR = 7.3, 95% CI: 3.66–14.72; p < 0.001), unfriendly health worker attitude (aOR = 5.8, 95% CI: 1.29–11.46; p = 0.022), and vaccine stock-outs (aOR = 6.1, 95% CI: 1.71–14.67; p = 0.005). Children of caregivers aged 25–34 years were less likely to have incomplete immunization than those of caregivers aged over 34 years (aOR = 0.3, 95% CI: 0.16–0.76; p = 0.008).
解释与创新
该研究确定了护理人员和卫生系统层面的可改变决定因素是与未完成免疫接种相关的重要因素。
科研与公卫意义
迫切需要有针对性的干预措施,包括护理人员教育、及时产后 疫苗接种、卫生工作者培训和加强 疫苗 供应。
局限与证据强度
该研究是在乌干达的一家医院进行的,这可能会限制普遍性。证据是温和的,因为它基于横截面设计和自我报告的数据。

Prevalence and factors associated with immunization non-completion among children aged 12–23 months at Kisoro General Hospital, Uganda: a cross-sectional study

Authors: Grace Acomo, James Nelson Okema, Ronald Obal, Faith Akumu Foscah, Ikiriza Mary Lourdes, Martin John Sesay, Fonti Bangura, Joel Bulitya, John LR Elioba
Original abstract (English)
Immunization non-completion, defined here as a child missing at least one age-appropriate vaccine dose under Uganda’s national immunization schedule (UNEPI), remains a critical public health problem in sub-Saharan Africa, exposing children to preventable diseases. In Uganda, coverage falls substantially below the WHO target of 90%, particularly in rural districts. This study determined the prevalence of immunization non-completion among children aged 12–23 months at Kisoro General Hospital and identified associated socio-economic and healthcare-related factors. A cross-sectional study was conducted from October to December 2024 at Kisoro General Hospital among 275 caregivers of children aged 12–23 months attending the immunization clinic, selected by systematic random sampling. Immunization non-completion was defined as failure to receive at least one age-appropriate dose per the UNEPI schedule (BCG, OPV, pentavalent, PCV, rotavirus, measles, and yellow fever vaccines) by 12–23 months of age. Data were collected via structured, pre-tested questionnaires and verified immunization card records. Descriptive statistics, bivariate chi-square tests, and multivariable logistic regression were performed using Stata version 15.1 (StataCorp, College Station, TX, USA). The participation rate was 90.5% (275/304). Caregivers who declined participation ( n = 10), were excluded after consent due to absent immunization records ( n = 12), or whose children fell outside the 12–23-month age range ( n = 7) are reported in full in the Results section. The prevalence of immunization non-completion was 34.2% (94/275; 95% CI: 28.5–39.8%). In multivariable analysis, significant predictors included lack of formal caregiver education (aOR = 3.3, 95% CI: 0.93–11.49; p = 0.044), delayed initiation of immunization beyond three weeks of birth (aOR = 7.3, 95% CI: 3.66–14.72; p < 0.001), unfriendly health worker attitude (aOR = 5.8, 95% CI: 1.29–11.46; p = 0.022), and vaccine stock-outs (aOR = 6.1, 95% CI: 1.71–14.67; p = 0.005). Children of caregivers aged 25–34 years were less likely to have incomplete immunization than those of caregivers aged over 34 years (aOR = 0.3, 95% CI: 0.16–0.76; p = 0.008). One in three children attending Kisoro General Hospital had incomplete immunization, far below national and global targets. Modifiable determinants at both caregiver and health system levels were identified. Targeted interventions including caregiver education, timely postnatal vaccination, health worker training, and vaccine supply strengthening are urgently needed.
View research seven-element analysis
Question and background
Immunization non-completion, defined here as a child missing at least one age-appropriate vaccine dose under Uganda’s national immunization schedule (UNEPI), remains a critical public health problem in sub-Saharan Africa, exposing children to preventable diseases.
Design and population
A cross-sectional study was conducted from October to December 2024 at Kisoro General Hospital among 275 caregivers of children aged 12–23 months attending the immunization clinic, selected by systematic random sampling.
Core methods
Data were collected via structured, pre-tested questionnaires and verified immunization card records. Descriptive statistics, bivariate chi-square tests, and multivariable logistic regression were performed using Stata version 15.1 (StataCorp, College Station, TX, USA).
Main results
The prevalence of immunization non-completion was 34.2% (94/275; 95% CI: 28.5–39.8%). In multivariable analysis, significant predictors included lack of formal caregiver education (aOR = 3.3, 95% CI: 0.93–11.49; p = 0.044), delayed initiation of immunization beyond three weeks of birth (aOR = 7.3, 95% CI: 3.66–14.72; p < 0.001), unfriendly health worker attitude (aOR = 5.8, 95% CI: 1.29–11.46; p = 0.022), and vaccine stock-outs (aOR = 6.1, 95% CI: 1.71–14.67; p = 0.005). Children of caregivers aged 25–34 years were less likely to have incomplete immunization than those of caregivers aged over 34 years (aOR = 0.3, 95% CI: 0.16–0.76; p = 0.008).
Interpretation and novelty
The study identified modifiable determinants at both caregiver and health system levels as significant factors associated with immunization non-completion.
Scientific and public-health significance
Targeted interventions including caregiver education, timely postnatal vaccination, health worker training, and vaccine supply strengthening are urgently needed.
Limitations and evidence strength
The study was conducted at a single hospital in Uganda, which may limit generalizability. The evidence is moderate as it is based on a cross-sectional design and self-reported data.

接种疫苗的病例是否会传播 麻疹?系统回顾和荟萃分析

Do vaccinated cases transmit measles? A systematic review and meta-analysis
作者： James Wright, Natasha S. Crowcroft, Julie Perry, Heili Poolsaar, Paul A. Gastañaduy, David N. Durrheim, William J. Moss, Susan Hahné, Walter A. Orenstein, Paul A. Rota
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该审查的重点是接种疫苗的个体是否可以传播麻疹病毒,并检查此类传播的特征,包括麻疹负担、剂量次数以及自疫苗接种以来的时间。核心问题是 麻疹 传播 是否来自接种疫苗的个体及其对公共健康的影响。
证据基础与方法
该审查对同行评审和灰色文献进行了系统检索,确定了 11,911 条记录,其中包括 33 篇文章。荟萃分析根据 麻疹 负担、剂量次数和自 疫苗接种 以来的时间计算了疫苗接种病例中 传播 的中位数。
共识与结论
来自接种疫苗的个体的 麻疹 传播 虽然相对不常见,但可能有助于 暴发。荟萃分析发现,消灭地区接种疫苗的传播者比非消灭地区的传播者年龄更大,91%的研究提供了后续传播世代的数据,每个接种疫苗的传播者中位数为4例。
争议与缺口
审查指出,某些领域的证据有限,特别是关于接种疫苗的传播者的年龄分布和 传播 的机制。还需要对 暴发 中接种疫苗的个体的作用进行更详细的研究。
科研与实践启示
公共卫生调查应考虑来自已接种疫苗的个体的 麻疹 传播,特别是在 麻疹 已被消除的地区。需要进一步的研究来了解疫苗接种病例中 传播 的动态并改进 暴发 的应对策略。

Do vaccinated cases transmit measles? A systematic review and meta-analysis

Authors: James Wright, Natasha S. Crowcroft, Julie Perry, Heili Poolsaar, Paul A. Gastañaduy, David N. Durrheim, William J. Moss, Susan Hahné, Walter A. Orenstein, Paul A. Rota
Original abstract (English)
INTRODUCTION: We reviewed evidence on whether vaccinated individuals can transmit measles and conducted a meta-analysis to understand transmission characteristics. METHODS: We searched and extracted data from peer-reviewed and gray literature and included studies reporting measles transmission events from vaccinated individuals. We meta-analyzed the data to calculate the median number of transmissions from vaccinated cases by measles burden, number of doses, and time since first and last dose. RESULTS: We identified 11,911 peer-reviewed and 22 gray literature records and included 33 articles in our review. Seventy individuals who had received 1 or more doses of measles-containing vaccine transmitted measles virus, resulting in 237 secondary cases. Vaccinated transmitters in eliminated areas were older (median age of 19 years (IQR: 3, 21)) than those in non-eliminated areas (median age 16 years; IQR 13, 18) (p = 0.12). Additionally, 91% (30/33) of studies provided data on subsequent transmission generations, leading to 812 measles cases traced back to vaccinated individuals, with a median of 4 (IQR: 1, 10) cases per vaccinated transmitter. CONCLUSION: Measles transmissions from vaccinated cases, although relatively uncommon, must be considered in public health investigations, as such transmissions can contribute to outbreaks.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review focuses on whether vaccinated individuals can transmit measles virus and examines the characteristics of such transmission, including measles burden, number of doses, and time since vaccination. The central question is whether measles transmission occurs from vaccinated individuals and its implications for public health.
Evidence base and method
The review conducted a systematic search of peer-reviewed and gray literature, identifying 11,911 records and including 33 articles. The meta-analysis calculated the median number of transmissions from vaccinated cases based on measles burden, number of doses, and time since vaccination.
Consensus and conclusions
Measles transmissions from vaccinated individuals, although relatively uncommon, can contribute to outbreaks. The meta-analysis found that vaccinated transmitters in eliminated areas were older than those in non-eliminated areas, and 91% of studies provided data on subsequent transmission generations, with a median of 4 cases per vaccinated transmitter.
Controversies and gaps
The review notes that the evidence is limited in some areas, particularly regarding the age distribution of vaccinated transmitters and the mechanisms of transmission. There is also a need for more detailed studies on the role of vaccinated individuals in outbreaks.
Research and practice implications
Public health investigations should consider measles transmissions from vaccinated individuals, especially in areas where measles has been eliminated. Further research is needed to understand the dynamics of transmission from vaccinated cases and to improve outbreak response strategies.

A 高优先级学术文献 · 综述A high priorityAcademic literature · Review

乌干达 麻疹 暴发 的运营驱动因素:多区 暴发 因果关系分析,2025 年 7 月至 2026 年 3 月

Operational drivers of measles outbreaks in Uganda: a multi-district outbreak causality analysis, July 2025– March 2026
作者： Sharon Namasambi, Richard Migisha, Collins Ankunda, Yasiini Nuwamanya, Pauline Achom, Nasif Matovu, Winfred Nakaweesi, Vianney John Kigongo, Michael Mutegeki, Benon Kwesiga
摘要中文翻译摘要 背景 尽管存在有效的 麻疹 麻疹,但乌干达的 麻疹 仍然存在,这表明持续存在的免疫差距和卫生系统弱点。我们进行了多区 麻疹 因果关系分析 (OCA),以确定 麻疹 麻疹 的计划和卫生系统贡献者,并为 麻疹 消除计划提供信息。方法 我们在世界卫生组织框架的指导下,对乌干达 12 个受影响地区(2025-2026 年)进行了横断面混合方法 OCA。我们审查了 麻疹 病例调​​查报告和三角测量结果,并对地区卫生团队、卫生工作者、麻疹 和免疫工作人员、社区领导、村卫生团队和护理人员进行定性访谈。我们对数据进行演绎分析,以确定因果路径和影响因素。研究结果分为四个预先指定的分析主题:免疫服务提供、护理人员获取和需求、监测 和病例检测以及 暴发 准备和响应。结果 12个区报告病例1302例,其中实验室确诊病例80例,疑似死亡10例(病死率0.77%);46.5% (n=606) 发生在 18-59 个月的儿童中。大多数病例 (65.3%) 发生在未接种疫苗的儿童中,而接种两种 麻疹-风疹 (MR) 剂量的儿童中则有 1.0% 发生。在各地区,MR2 实施不完整、外展活动不规则、固定地点 疫苗接种 不一致、对错过 疫苗接种 的儿童的跟踪薄弱以及交通和距离障碍导致了持续的免疫差距。临床怀疑度低、村卫生团队参与有限、实验室和 监测 信息瓶颈、缺乏预先制定的应对计划、延迟响应启动以及隔离能力不足,进一步限制了早期检测和控制。结论 麻疹 暴发 主要是由于错过了 疫苗接种,特别是两剂 MR 计划的不完整交付,再加上准入障碍、延迟病例检测和有限的 暴发 准备。加强常规 MR1 和 MR2 交付、有针对性的追赶 疫苗接种、与社区相关的 麻疹 以及具有明确激活触发器的预先部署的地区应对计划,对于缩小乌干达的免疫差距和加速消除 麻疹 至关重要。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
尽管存在有效的 疫苗,乌干达的 麻疹 暴发 仍然存在,这表明持续存在的免疫差距和卫生系统弱点。该研究的重点是确定 麻疹 麻疹 的计划和卫生系统贡献者,并告知 麻疹 消除计划。
证据基础与方法
在世界卫生组织框架的指导下,对乌干达 12 个受影响地区(2025-2026 年)进行了多地区 暴发 因果关系分析 (OCA)。该审查涉及对数据进行演绎分析,以确定因果路径和影响因素。
共识与结论
麻疹 暴发 的主要原因是错过了 麻疹-风疹 (MR) 计划,特别是两剂 麻疹-风疹 (MR) 方案的交付不完整,再加上准入障碍、延迟病例检测和有限的 麻疹 准备。研究结果分为四个预先指定的分析主题:免疫服务提供、护理人员获取和需求、监测 和病例检测以及 暴发 准备和响应。
争议与缺口
该研究发现了一些局限性,包括临床怀疑度低、村卫生团队的参与有限、实验室和 监测 信息瓶颈、缺乏预先制定的应对计划、响应启动延迟以及隔离能力不足。这些因素进一步限制了 暴发 的早期发现和控制。
科研与实践启示
加强常规 MR1 和 MR2 交付、有针对性的追赶 疫苗接种、与社区相关的 麻疹 以及具有明确激活触发器的预先部署的地区应对计划,对于缩小乌干达的免疫差距和加速消除 麻疹 至关重要。

Operational drivers of measles outbreaks in Uganda: a multi-district outbreak causality analysis, July 2025– March 2026

Authors: Sharon Namasambi, Richard Migisha, Collins Ankunda, Yasiini Nuwamanya, Pauline Achom, Nasif Matovu, Winfred Nakaweesi, Vianney John Kigongo, Michael Mutegeki, Benon Kwesiga
Original abstract (English)
Abstract Background Measles outbreaks in Uganda persist despite the availability of an effective vaccine, suggesting persistent immunity gaps and health system weaknesses. We conducted a multi-district outbreak causality analysis (OCA) to identify programmatic and health-system contributors to measles outbreaks and inform measles elimination programming. Methods We conducted a cross-sectional mixed-methods OCA across 12 affected districts in Uganda (2025–2026), guided by the World Health Organization framework. We reviewed measles case investigation reports and triangulated findings with qualitative interviews with district health teams, health workers, surveillance and immunisation staff, community leaders, Village Health Teams, and caregivers. We deductively analysed data to identify causal pathways and contributing factors. Findings were organized into four prespecified analytical themes: immunization service delivery, caregiver access and demand, surveillance and case detection, and outbreak preparedness and response. Results The 12 districts reported 1,302 cases, including 80 laboratory-confirmed cases and 10 suspected deaths (case-fatality rate: 0.77%); 46.5% (n=606) occurred among children aged 18– 59 months. Most cases (65.3%) occurred in unvaccinated children, versus 1.0% in children with both measles-rubella (MR) doses. Across districts, incomplete MR2 implementation, irregular outreach, inconsistent fixed-site vaccination, weak follow-up of children who missed vaccination, and transport and distance barriers contributed to persistent immunity gaps. Low clinical suspicion, limited engagement of Village Health Teams, laboratory and surveillance-information bottlenecks, absence of pre-positioned response plans, delayed response activation, and inadequate isolation capacity further limited early detection and control. Conclusion Measles outbreaks were driven primarily by missed vaccination, especially incomplete delivery of the two-dose MR schedule, compounded by access barriers, delayed case detection, and limited outbreak preparedness. Strengthening routine MR1 and MR2 delivery, targeted catch-up vaccination, community-linked surveillance, and pre-positioned district response plans with clear activation triggers will be critical to closing immunity gaps and accelerating measles elimination in Uganda.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Measles outbreaks in Uganda persist despite the availability of an effective vaccine, suggesting persistent immunity gaps and health system weaknesses. The study focuses on identifying programmatic and health-system contributors to measles outbreaks and informing measles elimination programming.
Evidence base and method
A multi-district outbreak causality analysis (OCA) was conducted across 12 affected districts in Uganda (2025–2026), guided by the World Health Organization framework. The review involved deductive analysis of data to identify causal pathways and contributing factors.
Consensus and conclusions
Measles outbreaks were driven primarily by missed vaccination, especially incomplete delivery of the two-dose measles-rubella (MR) schedule, compounded by access barriers, delayed case detection, and limited outbreak preparedness. Findings were organized into four prespecified analytical themes: immunization service delivery, caregiver access and demand, surveillance and case detection, and outbreak preparedness and response.
Controversies and gaps
The study identified several limitations, including low clinical suspicion, limited engagement of Village Health Teams, laboratory and surveillance-information bottlenecks, absence of pre-positioned response plans, delayed response activation, and inadequate isolation capacity. These factors further limited early detection and control of outbreaks.
Research and practice implications
Strengthening routine MR1 and MR2 delivery, targeted catch-up vaccination, community-linked surveillance, and pre-positioned district response plans with clear activation triggers will be critical to closing immunity gaps and accelerating measles elimination in Uganda.

巴勒斯坦西岸初级保健医生的疾病通知障碍:一项横断面调查

Barriers to disease notification among primary healthcare physicians in West Bank, Palestine: a cross-sectional survey
作者： Ayah Tuffaha, Emily White Johansson, Subhi Alshaer, Samer Asad
摘要中文翻译
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该审查调查了巴勒斯坦西岸初级保健医生的疾病通报障碍,重点关注 麻疹、腮腺炎和水痘这些应通报的疾病。核心问题涉及初级保健医生对于疑似 麻疹 病例通知的知识、态度和做法,包括与适当通知相关的因素和及时报告的障碍。
证据基础与方法
该审查报告了 2024 年 8 月至 2025 年 5 月在巴勒斯坦卫生部中心的初级保健医生中进行的一项横断面调查。该研究使用问卷来评估知识、态度和自我报告的通知实践,并应用多变量逻辑回归来识别与近期通知实践相关的因素。
共识与结论
在223名受访者中,30.9%的人对疾病通报流程了解良好,93.3%的人对通报态度良好。超过 80% 的人报告报告了他们最后一次遇到的 麻疹、腮腺炎或水痘病例,但及时报告的比例较低,只有 52.4% 的 麻疹 病例立即报告。常见的误解包括认为通知需要实验室确认(61.0%)以及 监测 病例定义需要实验室标准(65.5%)。
争议与缺口
知识和态度得分与通知实践没有显着相关,这可能是由于观察和综合评分方法有限。该审查强调,尽管态度积极,但仍存在持续漏报的情况,这表明存在尚未解决的障碍,例如缺乏时间(60.5%)、不确定要报告哪些疾病(38.1%)以及不确定何时报告(32.3%)。
科研与实践启示
The review suggests that strengthening training, clarifying guidance, improving feedback mechanisms, and addressing identified barriers may enhance disease notification performance by primary healthcare physicians in the West Bank. Specifically, physicians who received recent supervisory feedback had higher odds of notifying chickenpox cases (OR: 4.01, 95%CI: 1.40–11.43), and other medical specialties had lower odds of notifying mumps (OR: 0.21, 95%CI: 0.06–0.81) and chickenpox cases (OR: 0.23, 95%CI: 0.08–0.69).

Barriers to disease notification among primary healthcare physicians in West Bank, Palestine: a cross-sectional survey

Authors: Ayah Tuffaha, Emily White Johansson, Subhi Alshaer, Samer Asad
Original abstract (English)
Abstract Introduction Underreporting remains a persistent challenge for infectious disease surveillance systems. Evidence on notification knowledge and practices among physicians in the West Bank remains limited, although they play a critical role in disease notification. We aimed to assess the knowledge, attitudes, and practices of primary healthcare physicians in disease notification, identify factors associated with appropriate notification of suspected mumps, measles, and chickenpox cases, and describe barriers to notification. Methods We conducted a cross-sectional survey on primary healthcare physicians in the Palestinian Ministry of Health primary healthcare centers between August 2024 and May 2025. The questionnaire assessed knowledge, attitudes, and self-reported notification practices. Multivariable logistic regression was used to identify factors associated with recent notification practices. Results Among 223 respondents, 30.9% were categorized as having a good knowledge score of the disease notification process across multiple operational domains of disease notification, while 93.3% were categorized as having a good attitude score. Common misconceptions included laboratory confirmation being required for notification (61.0%) and that surveillance case definitions require laboratory criteria (65.5%). Over 80% of respondents notified their last-encountered measles, mumps, or chickenpox case. However, timely notification was lower; 52.4% of measles cases were reported immediately, compared with 19.3% of mumps and 14.0% of chickenpox cases reported within the recommended timeframes. Commonly reported barriers included lack of time (60.5%), and uncertainty about which diseases to notify (38.1%) and when (32.3%). Knowledge and attitude scores were not significantly associated with notification practices, although limited observations and composite scores may have obscured these associations. Physicians who received recent supervisory feedback on disease notification had higher odds of notifying chickenpox cases (OR: 4.01, 95%CI: 1.40-11.43). Compared to general practitioners, other medical specialties had lower odds of notifying mumps (OR: 0.21, 95%CI: 0.06–0.81) and chickenpox cases (OR: 0.23, 95%CI: 0.08–0.69). Conclusions Despite positive attitudes towards the notification system, knowledge gaps and misconceptions may contribute to underreporting of notifiable conditions. Strengthening training, clarifying guidance, improving feedback mechanisms, and addressing identified barriers and preferences may enhance disease notification performance by primary healthcare physicians in the West Bank.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review examines barriers to disease notification among primary healthcare physicians in the West Bank, Palestine, focusing on measles, mumps, and chickenpox as notifiable conditions. The central question addresses the knowledge, attitudes, and practices of primary healthcare physicians regarding notification of suspected measles cases, including factors associated with appropriate notification and barriers to timely reporting.
Evidence base and method
The review reports a cross-sectional survey conducted among primary healthcare physicians in Palestinian Ministry of Health centers from August 2024 to May 2025. The study used a questionnaire to assess knowledge, attitudes, and self-reported notification practices, and applied multivariable logistic regression to identify factors associated with recent notification practices.
Consensus and conclusions
Among 223 respondents, 30.9% had good knowledge of the disease notification process, while 93.3% demonstrated a good attitude toward notification. Over 80% reported notifying their last-encountered measles, mumps, or chickenpox case, but timely notification was lower, with only 52.4% of measles cases reported immediately. Common misconceptions included the belief that laboratory confirmation is required for notification (61.0%) and that surveillance case definitions require laboratory criteria (65.5%).
Controversies and gaps
Knowledge and attitude scores were not significantly associated with notification practices, potentially due to limited observations and composite scoring methods. The review highlights persistent underreporting despite positive attitudes, indicating unresolved barriers such as lack of time (60.5%), uncertainty about which diseases to notify (38.1%), and uncertainty about when to notify (32.3%).
Research and practice implications
The review suggests that strengthening training, clarifying guidance, improving feedback mechanisms, and addressing identified barriers may enhance disease notification performance by primary healthcare physicians in the West Bank. Specifically, physicians who received recent supervisory feedback had higher odds of notifying chickenpox cases (OR: 4.01, 95%CI: 1.40–11.43), and other medical specialties had lower odds of notifying mumps (OR: 0.21, 95%CI: 0.06–0.81) and chickenpox cases (OR: 0.23, 95%CI: 0.08–0.69).

Britannica Editors Last updated Aug. 11, 2026 • History Top Questions What is measles? Measles is a highly contagious viral disease, a hallmark symptom of which is a blotchy rash. It is most common in children and can be prevented through vaccination. What happens if you get measles? If you get measles, you are likely to develop a high fever , cough , and runny nose and have red, watery eyes. These symptoms are followed by a characteristic rash that spreads from the face downward. Symptoms appear 7–14 days after exposure and can lead to severe complications, including pneumonia , encephalitis ( brain inflammation ), diarrhea , or death, particularly in people who are unvaccinated. Can you get measles if you had a vaccine? It is possible, though rare, to get measles even after being vaccinated. Such breakthrough infections occur because the MMR vaccine , which protects against measles, is about 97 percent effective after two doses. However, vaccinated individuals who become infected usually experience a milder illness and are less likely to spread it to others, providing crucial protection for vulnerable populations, including the very young and the elderly. Why is measles coming back? Measles is resurging primarily due to declining vaccination rates, which have dropped below the 95 percent threshold required for herd immunity . Increased vaccine hesitancy and interruptions in routine immunization schedules, particularly during the COVID-19 pandemic , have been major contributing factors to this decline. Because the measles virus is highly contagious, it spreads easily and quickly through under-vaccinated communities. What is the recommended treatment for measles? No drug is effective against measles. The only treatment required is control of fever, rest in bed, protection of the eyes, care of the bowels, and sometimes steam inhalations to relieve irritation of the bronchial tree. When no complications occur, the illness lasts 10 days. What are complications of measles? Measles complications are frequent and include superimposed bacterial ear infection, pneumonia, and lung infection. The virus can also cause hepatitis, appendicitis, and gangrene of the extremities. Encephalitis is a rare occurrence. Deaths attributed to measles usually result from secondary bronchopneumonia caused by bacterial organisms entering the inflamed bronchial tree. Can measles affect adults? Measles is most common in children but may appear in older persons who escaped it earlier in life. Immunity to measles following an attack is usually lifelong. Is there a vaccine for measles? Measles vaccines are live vaccines that work against measles alone or in combination with other agents, specifically with rubella (MR), mumps and rubella (MMR), or mumps, rubella, and varicella (MMRV). The vaccines are typically given in two doses, the first before 15 months of age and the second dose later; timing of the dosages varies by country or region. News • Correction: Trump-Vaccines story • Aug. 11, 2026, 10:09 AM ET (AP) measles , contagious viral disease marked by fever , cough , conjunctivitis , and a characteristic rash. Measles is most common in children but may appear in older persons who escaped it earlier in life. Infants are immune up to four or five months of age if the mother has had the disease . Immunity to measles following an attack is usually lifelong. The disease can be prevented through immunization with the MMR vaccine . Learn more in this list of vaccine-preventable diseases . Transmission and symptoms Measles Giant cell in a patient with measles. Measles is so highly communicable that the slightest contact with an active case may infect a susceptible person. After an incubation period of about 10 days, the patient develops fever, redness and watering of the eyes, profuse nasal discharge , and congestion of the mucous membrane s of the nose and throat—symptoms often mistaken for those of a severe cold. This period of invasion lasts for 48 to 96 hours. The fever increases with appearance of a blotchy rash , and the temperature may rise as high as 40 °C (about 105 °F) when the rash reaches its maximum. Twenty-four to 36 hours before the rash develops, there appear in the mucous membranes of the mouth typical maculae, called Koplik spots —bluish white specks surrounded by bright red areas about 1 / 32 inch (0.75 mm) in diameter. After a day or two the rash becomes a deeper red and gradually fades, the temperature drops rapidly, and the catarrhal symptoms disappear. Britannica Quiz A Visit with the Word Doctor: Medical Vocabulary Quiz Treatment and complications No drug is effective against measles. The only treatment required is control of fever, rest in bed, protection of the eyes, care of the bowels, and sometimes steam inhalations to relieve irritation of the bronchial tree. When no complications occur, the illness lasts 10 days. Although uncomplicated measles is seldom fatal, infection with the virus has been shown to induce a form of “immune amnesia,” whereby measles virus eliminates as many as half of the antibodies generated against other infectious agents to which an individual was exposed previously. Thus, persons who survive measles infection may become vulnerable once again to a range of other diseases, such as chickenpox and polio . By contrast, individuals vaccinated against measles do not experience a loss of immunity to other infectious agents. Deaths attributed to measles usually result from secondary bronchopneumonia caused by bacterial organisms entering the inflamed bronchial tree. Complications of measles are frequent and include a superimposed bacterial ear infection or pneumonia or a primary measles lung infection. Encephalitis is a rare occurrence. Measles virus can invade various organ systems and cause hepatitis , appendicitis , and gangrene of the extremities. A large percentage of cases of severe measles are associated with inadequate intake of vitamin A , and there is evidence that treatment with vitamin A may reduce measles complications. On very rare occasions, persistent infection with a mutant measles virus can cause a degenerative central nervous system disease called subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), in which there is a gradual onset of progressive behavioral and intellectual deterioration. Motor incoordination and impairment of speech and sight subsequently develop. The final stages of stupor, dementia, blindness, and death occur within six to nine months. There is no treatment for SSPE. Measles vaccine and eradication efforts How We Develop New Vaccines Worried about diphtheria? No? Thank vaccines. See all videos for this article Mortality caused by measles declined steadily in the 20th century as the health of children and infants improved and effective treatment of complications became possible through the use of sulfonamide and antibiotic drugs. The widespread use of measles vaccine , beginning in the late 1960s, raised hopes for the eventual eradication of the disease. In the following decades, however, measles remained a leading cause of childhood mortality worldwide, primarily because attack rates remained high in less-developed countries, where factors such as malnutrition and weak public health infrastructure challenged infant health and the establishment of immunization programs. In the early 21st century, campaigns were initiated to increase vaccination , particularly in less-developed countries. International efforts led to a significant reduction in measles cases and deaths, bringing global elimination of the disease within reach. At the same time, supporting this progress, many wealthier countries, including the United States and some countries in the European Union , had successfully eradicated measles. Explore Britannica Premium! The trusted destination for professionals, college students, and lifelong learners. SUBSCRIBE In the second decade of the 21st century, however, large measles outbreaks continued to occur in countries with low vaccination rates. In late 2019 in Samoa , for instance, a severe outbreak of measles resulted in school closures and government shutdown, followed by a mass vaccination campaign to prevent further spread of the disease. Measles also reemerged in several countries where it previously had been eradicated, a trend attributed to alarming declines in vaccination coverage. Of particular concern was the return of measles in developed countries such as the United States, where the disease had been eliminated by 2000, and the United Kingdom. In these countries, measles reemerged in the form of small outbreaks that were typically concentrated in areas with relatively high proportions of unvaccinated individuals. In early 2025, the death of a child amid a measles outbreak in Texas marked the country’s first death caused by measles in more than a decade. Coincident with the return of measles in more developed countries, however, was a massive surge in the disease in countries with traditionally low vaccination coverage, resulting in large increases in measles cases worldwide. Global incidence was notably high in 2019, with more than 364,800 cases reported between June and July alone that year—far exceeding the number of cases reported worldwide over that time frame for any year since 2006, according to officials with the World Health Organization (WHO). The largest increases occurred in countries in Africa, Europe, and the Western Pacific. By early 2020 the Democratic Republic of the Congo was in the midst of a severe measles epidemic , with more than 6,000 deaths according to WHO. WHO and health officials in regions affected by measles outbreaks increased efforts to bolster vaccination rates to stop the disease from spreading further. Find out from Dr. Tina Tan of Northwestern University why adult vaccination against various types of diseases is important Learn about the importance of adult vaccination against measles and other diseases. See all videos for this article Measles vaccines are live vaccines that work against measles alone or in combination against other agents, specifically with rubella ( MR ), mumps and rubella ( MMR ), or mumps, rubella, and varicella ( MMRV ). The vaccines typically are given in two doses. In the United States, for example, the first dose is given at 12 to 15 months of age, and the second dose is recommended at four to six years. In other countries, the vaccine is given first at nine months and the second dose later. The second dose of MMR must be given at least four weeks after the first dose; in adults whose vaccination status is uncertain, the two doses typically are given four weeks apart. The youngest age at which the vaccines can be given is six months, though revaccination (with two doses) is needed later. See MMR vaccine . Also called: rubeola Key People: Maurice Hilleman Guillaume de Baillou Related Topics: Koplik spot MMRV vaccine Morbillivirus MR vaccine subacute sclerosing panencephalitis See all related content Similar illnesses Measles must be differentiated from other disorders accompanied by an eruption. In roseola infantum , a disease seen in babies, a measleslike rash appears after the child has had a high temperature for two or three days, but there is no fever at the time of the rash. German measles ( rubella ) can be superficially differentiated from measles by the shorter course of the disease and mildness of the symptoms. Sometimes the rashes of scarlet fever , serum reactions, and other conditions may, on certain parts of the body, look like measles. Drugs that may produce rashes similar to measles are phenobarbital, diphenylhydantoin, the sulfonamides, phenolphthalein, and penicillin . Measles Outbreaks! How America Resurrected a Defeated Disease - The People's Pharmacy
新闻简报The United States has experienced a significant measles outbreak in 2026, with the CDC confirming 2,318 cases as of July 23, surpassing the total number of cases reported in all of 2025. This marks the highest number of measles cases in the country in 35 years, with 35 outbreaks and 45 jurisdictions reporting cases. Nearly half of the cases involved children and teenagers aged 5 to 19, and 20 percent were children under 5. While there have been no reported deaths in 2026, three deaths were recorded in 2025, including two previously healthy children. The outbreak has raised concerns about underreporting, as many cases may go untested or misdiagnosed, especially in communities with vaccine hesitancy. Additionally, measles can lead to severe complications such as pneumonia, encephalitis, and even subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a rare but fatal neurological condition. The CDC estimates that SSPE may develop in seven to 11 individuals per 100,000 cases, with higher risks for children infected before age 2. The long-term consequences of this outbreak could become apparent as early as the 2030s, highlighting the need for continued vigilance and vaccination efforts.

Nearly half of all cases occurred among children and teenagers aged 5 to 19, and 20 percent involved children under 5. Measles Outbreaks! How America Resurrected a Defeated Disease - The People's Pharmacy

News Brief
The United States has experienced a significant measles outbreak in 2026, with the CDC confirming 2,318 cases as of July 23, surpassing the total number of cases reported in all of 2025. This marks the highest number of measles cases in the country in 35 years, with 35 outbreaks and 45 jurisdictions reporting cases. Nearly half of the cases involved children and teenagers aged 5 to 19, and 20 percent were children under 5. While there have been no reported deaths in 2026, three deaths were recorded in 2025, including two previously healthy children. The outbreak has raised concerns about underreporting, as many cases may go untested or misdiagnosed, especially in communities with vaccine hesitancy. Additionally, measles can lead to severe complications such as pneumonia, encephalitis, and even subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), a rare but fatal neurological condition. The CDC estimates that SSPE may develop in seven to 11 individuals per 100,000 cases, with higher risks for children infected before age 2. The long-term consequences of this outbreak could become apparent as early as the 2030s, highlighting the need for continued vigilance and vaccination efforts.
Original source text (English)
The United States has broken another record. Unfortunately, this is not one anyone should celebrate. As of July 23, 2026, the CDC had confirmed 2,318 cases of measles in the United States. That is already more than the 2,289 cases reported during all of 2025, with more than five months still remaining in the year. Measles outbreaks are back with a vengeance! This is the largest number of measles cases the country has recorded in 35 years. The last time the total was higher was 1991, when 9,643 cases were reported. That was nine years before the United States declared measles eliminated. The obvious question is: How did this country resurrect a disease it had already defeated? Measles Outbreaks Set a 35-Year Record The latest CDC numbers are sobering: - 2,318 confirmed measles cases in 2026 - 35 newly reported outbreaks - 45 jurisdictions reporting cases - 151 hospitalizations - 93 percent of cases linked to outbreaks - 93 percent of patients unvaccinated or of unknown vaccination status - 69 percent of patients younger than 20 Nearly half of all cases have occurred among children and teenagers between 5 and 19 years old. Another 20 percent have involved children under 5. There have been no reported measles deaths in the United States so far this year. That is welcome news, but it should not be interpreted to mean the disease is harmless. Three Americans died from measles in 2025, including two previously healthy children. Of this year’s 456 patients under age 5, 45 have required hospitalization. Among adults with measles, approximately one in ten has been hospitalized. How Accurate if the FDA’s Measles Count? Do you trust the CDC? There have been substantial staff cuts. This is partially a political issue because anything associated with vaccination these days is political. We suspect that the measles outbreaks, as dramatic as they seem, are undercounting actual cases. Families may not seek medical attention for a child who is sick. That may be especially true in religious communities where there is some reluctance to engage with conventional medicine or public health authorities. Many patients may not be tested. Some cases may be mild or atypical. Since most physicians have never seen measles before, they might misdiagnosis it not report it. “Measles Was No Big Deal When I Was a Child” Whenever we write about measles outbreaks, readers remind us that nearly everyone used to catch the disease. C. offered this memory: “Back in the days of my youth, measles was a ‘rite of passage,’ i.e., everyone had measles, mumps and rubella. Yes, some kids were disabled to various degrees but not that many compared to all the kids who were infected. Measles was part of life.” Ron agreed: “When I and my children were kids, getting the measles was nothing major. In fact, usually, kids would get them together. It really was not a big problem.” We understand why people remember measles that way. Before the vaccine became available in 1963, almost every American child eventually caught it. Most survived without an obvious lasting disability. But that does not mean measles was benign. Before vaccination, the CDC estimates that 3 million to 4 million Americans were infected every year. Roughly 48,000 were hospitalized, 1,000 developed encephalitis and between 400 and 500 died annually. The disease seemed ordinary because it was common—not because it was safe. Measles Outbreaks Cause More Than a Rash Measles usually begins with high fever, cough, runny nose and red, watery eyes. Tiny white spots called Koplik spots may appear inside the mouth. A blotchy red rash then spreads from the face down the body. That description can make measles sound like chickenpox with a worse cold. It is not. Measles can cause: - Pneumonia - Severe dehydration - Ear infections - Hearing loss - Encephalitis, or inflammation of the brain - Permanent neurological injury - Death Children under 5, adults over 20, pregnant women and people with weakened immune systems are especially vulnerable to severe complications. There is also a rare, delayed complication with a name few people have ever heard: subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, or SSPE. A child can seemingly recover completely from measles and remain well for years. Then, usually seven to ten years later, persistent measles virus in the brain can trigger progressive neurological deterioration. SSPE may begin with behavioral changes, poor school performance or involuntary movements. It can progress to seizures, loss of vision, dementia, coma and death. It is almost always fatal. The CDC estimates that SSPE develops in roughly seven to 11 people for every 100,000 reported measles cases. The risk may be substantially higher when children catch measles before age 2. In other words, the final consequences of the 2026 measles outbreaks may not become fully visible until the 2030s. Measles Can Erase Part of the Immune System’s Memory One of the strangest consequences of measles is something scientists call immune amnesia. The immune system normally remembers bacteria and viruses it has encountered before. That memory helps the body respond more rapidly the next time a familiar germ appears. Measles can destroy some of those memory cells. After the rash and fever disappear, a child may look completely recovered. But the immune system can remain less capable of recognizing other infectious threats for months or even years. That means the damage from measles may extend beyond the measles infection itself. A child could become more susceptible to influenza, pneumonia or other illnesses that the immune system previously knew how to fight. It is almost as though measles breaks into the immune system’s library and tears pages out of its reference books. Why Measles Outbreaks Spread Like Wildfire Measles is one of the most contagious infections known. If one person with measles enters a room containing ten susceptible people, as many as nine may become infected. The virus spreads through the air when someone breathes, coughs or sneezes. Viral particles can remain suspended for as long as two hours after the infected person has left. People can also spread measles before they realize they are sick. An infected person is contagious for approximately four days before the rash appears and four days afterward. That makes contact tracing enormously difficult. A person could fly through a busy airport, eat in a restaurant or sit in a medical waiting room before the telltale rash develops. By the time measles is diagnosed, hundreds or even thousands of people may have been exposed. An investigation published in The Journal of Infectious Diseases (Feb. 27, 2026) illustrates just how easily measles outbreaks can spread in the modern travel era.One infected traveler who arrived at Denver International Airport in May 2025 was ultimately connected with at least 17 cases in multiple states. Public health investigators had to evaluate approximately 1,400 potential contacts. A single passenger had set off a multistate public-health scramble. Measles Outbreaks Pose a New Problem: Who Will Recognize It? There is another reason today’s measles outbreaks are so difficult to contain: Most Americans—including many healthcare professionals—have never seen a case. Sixty or 80 years ago, a worried mother, an experienced family doctor or even a school nurse might have recognized measles almost immediately. The combination of a high fever, hacking cough, runny nose, red watery eyes and a rash spreading downward from the face would have been painfully familiar. Today, measles may be the last thing anyone suspects. Parents may assume their child has the flu, COVID, an allergic reaction or some other viral rash. A medical resident or emergency physician may have studied measles in a textbook but never encountered it in an exam room. Because measles became so uncommon in the United States, healthcare providers may have a low level of suspicion and delay the correct diagnosis. That delay can have serious consequences. A child with fever and cough may spend hours in a crowded waiting room before the rash is recognized. By then, patients, parents, nurses, receptionists and other staff members may have been exposed. Because measles virus can linger in the air for up to two hours, people may enter the room long after the sick child has left and still become infected. That is why anyone who suspects measles should call the doctor, urgent-care center or emergency department before arriving. The facility can arrange for the patient to enter without sitting in a common waiting area and can take appropriate isolation precautions. Healthcare professionals are advised to think of measles when a patient has fever and rash along with cough, runny nose or red eyes—especially after international travel, exposure to another person with a rash or residence in a community experiencing an outbreak. Once measles is suspected, the patient should be masked and isolated immediately. Clinicians should contact the local health department and collect samples for laboratory confirmation. A disease can become unfamiliar without becoming harmless. In fact, forgetting what measles looks like gives this extraordinarily contagious virus one more opportunity to spread. The Hidden Price of Measles Outbreaks Hospitalizations are only one part of the damage. When a student is exposed to measles, health departments may require an unvaccinated child to remain home for weeks. Parents miss work. Schools must identify contacts and review immunization records. Medical centers isolate suspected patients and trace everyone who may have shared the air. The major South Carolina outbreak that began in 2025 ultimately produced about 1,000 cases. Dozens of schools were affected, and hundreds of people were quarantined. For families, that can mean missed classes, lost wages, unexpected childcare problems and enormous anxiety. The Actual Price of Measles You could easily be paying for measles. Researchers at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health reviewed the cost of responding to measles outbreaks in 18 states. They estimated an average cost of approximately $43,000 for every measles case, although the figure ranged from under $7,000 to more than $243,000. Those estimates include medical care and public-health work such as laboratory testing, vaccination clinics, contact tracing and emergency response. They do not fully capture lost productivity, disrupted education, caregiver time or the lifelong costs of neurological injury. No one writes a $43,000 check when a child develops measles. The expense is scattered among hospitals, health departments, schools, employers, insurers, families and taxpayers. That makes the cost easy to overlook—but it will affect more people than is generally appreciated. Why Are Measles Outbreaks Returning? The United States did not eradicate measles in 2000. It eliminated endemic transmission. Elimination meant that measles was no longer circulating continuously inside the country. Travelers could still contract the virus abroad and bring it home. For many years, those imported infections usually fizzled out. The virus encountered too few susceptible people to keep spreading. That protective barrier is now developing big cracks. The CDC reports that MMR vaccination coverage among American kindergartners fell from 95.2 percent during the 2019-2020 school year to 92.5 percent during 2024-2025. That may sound like a small decline, but measles is so contagious that communities generally need vaccination rates around 95 percent to prevent sustained transmission. The national average also conceals large local pockets where vaccination rates are much lower. Once measles reaches one of those communities, an imported case can become an outbreak. Vaccines can become victims of their own success. When a disease disappears, parents no longer see children struggling to breathe, developing brain inflammation or dying from the infection. The risk from the vaccine may begin to feel more immediate than the disease it prevents. Eventually, vaccine coverage declines enough for the disease to return. Then everyone is reminded why the vaccine was developed in the first place. Are Immigrants Responsible for Measles Outbreaks? Whenever we report on measles, some readers blame immigrants. Measles certainly can enter the country through international travel. But the southern border has been closed for more than a year. And yet cases have climbed to unprecedented levels this year. The overwhelming majority of patients have been U.S. residents. The large wave of infections in 2025 began in Texas and New Mexico. Investigators traced many early cases to a tight-knit Mennonite community in West Texas, where vaccination rates were low. Similar patterns have been observed in some Amish and ultra-Orthodox Jewish communities, as well as certain other religious or cultural groups where vaccination is discouraged. Vaccine hesitancy has also grown in parts of the broader population where distrust of public health institutions has increased. Tourists can bring measles into the US and Americans who vacation, work or study abroad can also bring measles back with them. Once measles arrives, the key question is not the traveler’s citizenship. It is whether the virus encounters enough susceptible people to continue spreading. A wall cannot stop an airborne virus carried home by an American tourist. Does the MMR Vaccine Work to Stop Measles Outbreaks? One dose of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine is about 93 percent effective against measles. Two doses are about 97 percent effective. No vaccine works perfectly. A small number of fully vaccinated people will still become infected, especially during a large outbreak with intense exposure. The 2026 numbers, however, show a striking pattern: - 93 percent of patients were unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination history. - 3 percent had received one MMR dose. - 4 percent had received two doses. The existence of breakthrough cases does not demonstrate that vaccination is useless. It shows that a very effective vaccine is not an invisible force field. Seat belts do not prevent every automobile death either. That does not make driving without one a wise experiment. Do Adults Need Another Measles Vaccination? This is where advice can become confusing. People born before 1957 are generally presumed to have immunity because nearly everyone was exposed to measles before the vaccine era. Some adults vaccinated between 1963 and 1967 received an older killed-virus vaccine that did not provide adequate protection. People who know they received that vaccine—or who received an unknown type of measles vaccine before 1968—should discuss revaccination with a healthcare professional. Most people who received the modern live-virus MMR vaccine according to the recommended schedule are considered protected for life against measles. Routine boosters are not generally recommended simply because many years have passed. People at increased risk, including international travelers, healthcare workers, college students and those living in an outbreak area, may need two documented doses. A blood test can also look for evidence of measles immunity, although public-health guidance generally favors vaccination when records are unavailable and there is no medical contraindication. There is no known harm from receiving another MMR dose if someone is already immune. Because MMR is a live-virus vaccine, however, it is not appropriate for everyone. Pregnant women and some people with severely weakened immune systems should not receive it. Such individuals should consult a knowledgeable healthcare professional rather than making the decision on their own. Why Vaccines Matter to Me Personally Please permit a brief personal note. As a very young child, I was hospitalized with polio at the Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia. I was placed in traction in an isolation ward. My parents could not visit me. Children around me were dying. It was terrifying, and the experience left emotional scars that have lasted a lifetime. A few years later, I developed pertussis, better known as whooping cough. I was extremely ill for a long time. Even today, a respiratory infection can trigger severe coughing spells that sound frightening. People who never witnessed polio, diphtheria, measles or whooping cough may find such diseases hard to imagine. That is understandable. Vaccination made them largely invisible. But invisible is not the same as extinct. Viruses and bacteria do not surrender. They wait for an opportunity. What the Record Measles Outbreaks Are Telling Us Compared with heart disease, cancer or dementia, 2,318 measles cases may seem like a trivial number. That misses the warning. Measles is sending up a flare. It is telling us that the country’s protective immunization barrier is weakening. When vaccination coverage declines, measles is often one of the first diseases to break through because it spreads so easily. Pertussis and other preventable infections may follow. The return of measles is not merely a story about one childhood rash. It is evidence that the United States is moving backward against diseases it once brought under control. Public-health officials will not restore trust by scolding skeptical parents or pretending vaccines are completely risk-free. Families deserve honest information, respectful answers and acknowledgement of genuine uncertainties. But they also deserve the truth about the diseases. Measles Outbreaks Will Not Disappear on Their Own Unless confidence in the MMR vaccine improves and vaccination coverage remains strong, measles outbreaks will continue to flare and spread. You can learn more about vaccine effectiveness at this link. Vaccines are not perfect, and like any medical intervention they can produce side effects in some people. But the protection they provide has prevented enormous suffering from d...

B routine priorityPublic-health news

At least 6 kids sickened by measles in Delaware as outbreak grows - delawareonline.com

News Brief
Delaware health officials reported a measles outbreak with 20 confirmed cases as of August 2026, all located in Kent County. Six cases occurred in children aged nine and under, one in a 10–19-year-old, and 13 in adults aged 20 and older. The Delaware Division of Public Health is leading the investigation, while state and national health authorities warn that measles is highly contagious and can cause severe complications. Post-exposure vaccination within three days and immunoglobulin within six days are recommended for unvaccinated or non-immune contacts. The outbreak coincides with a nationwide surge in measles cases in 2026, surpassing the total reported in 2025, amid declining vaccination coverage and waning herd immunity in several states.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Life Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals HEALTH At least 6 kids sickened by measles in Delaware as outbreak grows Isabel Hughes Delaware News Journal Aug. 12, 2026, 6:56 a.m. ET Measles cases in Delaware have reached double digits, with 20 people in the state testing positive for the highly-contagious virus as of Aug. 11. The majority of cases − 13 − are in residents age 20 and older, according to the Delaware Division of Public Health. One case was reported in a Delawarean between 10 and 19 years old, while the remaining six cases were in children 9 and under. All confirmed cases are in Kent County. Delaware's outbreak comes as measles cases continue to rise nationwide. According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, the number of U.S. cases reported in 2026 has already surpassed the total reported in all of 2025. Here's what to know about measles. What are measles? Measles is a highly contagious, acute viral illness that begins with early symptoms of fever, cough, runny nose and conjunctivitis (pink eye), lasting two to four days before rash onset. The rash typically occurs three to five days after symptoms begin and usually appears on the face and spreads down the body. Measles can cause severe health complications, including pneumonia, brain inflammation and death. The virus is transmitted by direct contact with infectious droplets or by airborne spread when an infected person breathes, coughs or sneezes. The measles virus can remain infectious in the air and on surfaces for up to two hours after an infected person leaves an area. Infected people are contagious from four days before the rash starts through four days after rash development. The incubation period for measles from exposure to fever is usually about 10 days, with a range of seven to 12 days. And from exposure to rash onset is usually about 14 days with a seven to 21-day range.More: Back‐to‐school vaccines: what DE requires before school year starts How do you treat measles? There is no specific antiviral cure for the measles. However, if you haven't been previously vaccinated against the measles, you can still be vaccinated to head off the worst symptoms, if you act fast enough. "If people older than six months of age are exposed to measles and are not immune by previous illness or vaccination, they can get a measles vaccine for up to three days after contact with the virus," advised the Mayo Clinic. "After the vaccine you don't need to stay home, also called quarantine." Antibodies can also be given for up to six days after exposure, though they are prioritized for people who cannot get the measles vaccine, such as infants younger than 6 months old, pregnant women, severely immunocompromised people or people who have other medical contraindications to vaccination, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Who should be most worried about measles? Jennifer Horney, professor and chair of the University of Delaware's Department of Epidemiology, said she believes the biggest concern should be parents of children who can't be vaccinated yet. "That's one of the things that people who make decisions not to vaccinate don't understand − is that you're making a decision for a lot of people (by not vaccinating)," she said. How contagious is measles? Per Horney, each person who is infected will likely spread to about 18 susceptible people. "So, if we compare that to something like COVID or flu that we're more familiar with, you would typically spread to maybe two susceptible people," Horney said. "If someone is on your plane and they have measles, if you're not vaccinated, you are going to get measles. It just is very, very effective at spreading, and you can spread before you have the rash." Who is most at risk during a measles outbreak? People who are unvaccinated, pregnant, immunocompromised or too young to be vaccinated face the highest risk of severe complications, DPH said. The first dose for infants is usually given between 12 and 15 months. Anyone who cannot get the vaccine but who has come into contact with someone suspected to have measles should immediately contact their primary care provider or Delaware's Office of Infectious Disease Epidemiology at (302) 744-4990 for guidance. Given the Delaware measles outbreak, can my infant get an early dose of the MMR vaccine? Speak to your pediatrician. Babies six months and older are physically able to receive the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine, though an early dose − defined as before 12 months of age − won't count toward their regular vaccine schedule, which is typically two doses. The second dose is generally given to kids between 4 and 6 years of age. Are adults who were vaccinated decades ago still protected? If you were born before 1957, you are considered immune. If you were fully vaccinated, you’re also considered immune. If you’re concerned, however, your doctor can order a blood test to check for immunity. Such testing is fairly common. Why are measles on the rise in the United States? The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to the CDC. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. Vaccination for measles has declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated and most people are protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventives, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of the Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. USA TODAY contributed to this report. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ohio measles outbreak cases rise again, surpass last year's numbers - Akron Beacon Journal

News Brief
Ohio’s third measles outbreak of 2026 continues to expand, with state health officials reporting 69 confirmed cases across 11 counties as of August 7—surpassing the 45 cases recorded in 2025. The outbreak spans communities from Ashtabula to Wayne, with exposures linked to public venues including livestock auctions and retail locations. Nationally, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, with 2,295 infections reported so far in 2026, driven by declining vaccination rates that have eroded herd immunity. Ohio’s MMR coverage for children and adolescents stood at 91.5% in 2023, below the 95% threshold needed to prevent sustained spread. Health authorities are urging vaccination, noting that two doses of MMR are 97% effective, while warning that measles can cause severe complications including pneumonia and encephalitis, particularly in young children and immunocompromised individuals.
Original source text (English)
Akron mayor addresses firing of officer, settlement with Tucker estate News Sports Entertainment Lifestyle Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals HEALTHCARE Ohio measles outbreak cases rise again, surpass last year's numbers Chad Murphy James Powel Mary Walrath-Holdridge USA TODAY NETWORK Aug. 13, 2026, 3:17 p.m. ET Ohio's third measles outbreak of 2026 continues to grow, spreading to another county and surpassing the total number of cases from 2025 at the last count. Harrison County Public Health reported a measles case there on Aug. 12. Statewide, the Ohio Department of Health is reporting 69 cases in 11 counties, according to its summary of infectious diseases dashboard, updated Aug. 13. That's up from 45 cases in 10 counties on Aug. 6. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Nationwide, measles cases nationwide hit a 35-year high for a disease once declared eradicated in the United States. Data released July 22 by the Johns Hopkins University shows 2,295 measles cases have been reported nationwide so far this year, surpassing the 2,286 infections recorded during all of 2025 and marking the highest annual total since 1991. Measles growing in Ohio; counties reporting cases Ohio counties reporting measles cases since Jan. 1, 2026, include: Ashtabula: 17 Cuyahoga: 3 Franklin: 7 Geauga: 16 Harrison: 3 Holmes: 2 Knox: 1 Ottawa: 1 Trumbull: 2 Tuscarawas: 8 Wayne: 2 Unknown: 7 The latest case count reflects two previous outbreaks in Cuyahoga County in January and Franklin County in February. The ages of those with confirmed cases range from 15 months to 20 years old, according to various health departments. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with 45 in 2025, seven cases in 2024 and one case in 2023. Where are measles cases spreading? While Harrison County Public Health reported one case of measles as of Aug. 7, the state dashboard shows two additional cases there. The Wayne County Health Department reports some of its cases are likely associated with a previously identified measles exposure event at Smokey Lane Stables, 686 Smokey Lane Road NW in Sugarcreek, on July 17. One of the Tuscarawas cases is from the same event, that health department reports. Geauga Public Health reports that the individuals with measles visited several locations while able to spread the virus, including Bloomfield Horse Sale, 2211 Kinsman Road NW, on July 18. The Tuscarawas health department reports that another of its cases was exposed at the Bloomfield Livestock Auction, at the same address as Bloomfield Horse Sale, on on the same day. In Trumbull County, health officials report that infected individuals were at the Walmart in Liberty on July 29 and Akron Children's Mahoning Valley Emergency Department in Boardman on Aug. 2, during the time they were able to spread the virus. Measles cases hit 35-year high in United States The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to USA TODAY, citing the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. Health experts say the recent surge has been fueled by declining vaccination rates that have left more communities vulnerable to outbreaks. Why are measles cases on the rise? Ohio vaccination rates Vaccination for measles has declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated. Most people are then protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–20 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–25 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventives, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of the Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. Kennedy is now encouraging the measles vaccine ahead of back-to-school season. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023, the most recent year available from ODH. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Trump signs executive order to separate MMR vaccine President Donald Trump signed an executive order to split the childhood measles, mumps and rubella vaccine into three shots and delivering them at separate medical visits as well as "calling for more vaccine flexibility and research" and fewer childhood vaccinations, USA TODAY reports. "You have the MMR, we want it in three separate vaccinations, given at separate times. Together there could be a possibility they're quite lethal and separately, it looks like they are not at all lethal, but just very effective," said Trump from the Oval Office as he signed the order. But the MMR vaccine is not lethal, and medical research shows no health benefit or reduced risk from splitting it. In contrast, separating the shots increases injection pain, leaves children unprotected longer, and lowers overall vaccination rates, according to experts. Can you get measles if vaccinated? Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but it can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before, through four days after, a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. USA TODAY reporters James Powel and Mary Walrath-Holdridge contributed to this report. Top Doctors Say Mental Decline Begins When Seniors Can't Pronounce These 3 Phrases.
