威斯康星州 麻疹 暴发 确诊病例增至 15 例,疑似病例 6 例 - 《密尔沃基哨兵杂志》

Wisconsin measles outbreak grows to 15 confirmed cases, 6 probable - Milwaukee Journal Sentinel
新闻简报据《密尔沃基哨兵报》报道,威斯康星州西南部的 麻疹 暴发 病例已扩大至 15 例确诊病例和 6 例疑似病例。卫生官员认为当地存在活跃的 传播,表明社区中可能存在更多病例。暴发 是该州几十年来最严重的 麻疹 年份的一部分,2026 年记录了近 2,500 例病例。大多数感染者是未接种疫苗的儿童或青少年,由于 疫苗 怀疑情绪增加,该州的 麻疹 发病率一直在下降。官员们正在对可能暴露的人进行接触者追踪和外展活动,旨在迅速遏制 暴发。美国也正在经历自 1991 年以来最糟糕的 麻疹 年,全国报告了近 2,600 例病例。暴发 强调了低 疫苗接种 率的影响以及采取公共卫生干预措施以防止进一步传播的必要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发 于 2026 年 8 月 13 日报告,最新病例于该日确诊。活动日期并未明确说明,但 暴发 自 2026 年 8 月 14 日起正在进行。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在威斯康星州西南部,特别是爱荷华县和邻近的拉斐特县。受影响人群包括未接种疫苗的儿童和青少年,其中大多数病例年龄在 18 岁以下。
事件
威斯康星州西南部的 麻疹 麻疹 确诊病例已增至 15 例,疑似病例已增至 6 例。卫生官员怀疑社区中可能存在活跃的当地 传播 和更多病例。麻疹 是该州几十年来最糟糕的 麻疹 年份的一部分。
规模、影响与风险
麻疹 中有 15 例确诊病例和 6 例疑似 麻疹 病例。目前尚无住院治疗的报告。暴发 与 疫苗接种 发病率下降有关,超过 90% 的病例涉及未接种疫苗或未知 疫苗接种 状态的个人。该风险被认为是活跃的本地 传播,但没有明确说明风险级别。
应对、状态与不确定性
当地卫生官员正在对确诊病例进行访谈,以确定密切接触者,并联系那些可能接触过的人。他们还在评估 疫苗接种 的状态和症状。暴发 正在进行中,卫生官员怀疑可能存在更多病例,但尚未发现或报告。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Wisconsin measles outbreak grows to 15 confirmed cases, 6 probable - Milwaukee Journal Sentinel

News Brief
A measles outbreak in southwestern Wisconsin has expanded to 15 confirmed and six probable cases, according to the Milwaukee Journal Sentinel. Health officials believe there is active local transmission, suggesting more cases may be present in the community. The outbreak is part of the state's worst measles year in decades, with nearly 2,500 cases recorded in 2026. Most of the infected individuals were unvaccinated children or teens, and the state's vaccination rates for measles have been declining due to increasing vaccine skepticism. Officials are conducting contact tracing and outreach to those potentially exposed, aiming to contain the outbreak quickly. The U.S. is also experiencing its worst measles year since 1991, with nearly 2,600 cases reported nationwide. The outbreak highlights the impact of low vaccination rates and the need for public health interventions to prevent further spread.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Packers Business Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals NEWS Wisconsin measles outbreak grows to 15 confirmed cases, 6 probable Measles spreading in southwestern Wisconsin Sarah Volpenhein Milwaukee Journal Sentinel Aug. 13, 2026Updated Aug. 14, 2026, 12:41 p.m. CT A measles outbreak in southwestern Wisconsin has grown to 15 confirmed and six probable cases. Health officials believe there is active local transmission and more cases likely exist in the community. The U.S. is experiencing its worst measles year in decades, with nearly 2,500 cases recorded so far in 2026. Most of those infected in the Wisconsin outbreak were unvaccinated children or teens. An additional 13 cases of measles have been confirmed in Iowa County in southwestern Wisconsin, along with six probable cases, fueling an outbreak state health officials warn may still be spreading. The latest cases, reported on Aug. 13, bring the total number of outbreak cases to 15 confirmed and six probable, but health officials suspect more people may be infected. Two cases of measles were identified earlier this week in Iowa County and neighboring Lafayette County, and both people were believed to have been exposed in Wisconsin. "The rapid emergence of these cases, particularly among people who have not traveled outside of Wisconsin, indicates that there is active local transmission," said Ryan Westergaard, chief medical officer and state epidemiologist for communicable diseases for the Wisconsin Department of Health Services. "It is very likely that additional cases exist in the community that have not yet been identified or reported to public health," he said during a call with reporters Aug. 13. No public locations have been identified where people may have been exposed, Westergaard said. No hospitalizations related to the outbreak have been reported. U.S. dealing with worst measles year in decades The U.S. is dealing with its worst measles year in decades. The country has recorded nearly 2,600 measles cases so far in 2026, the most of any year since 1991, according to the federal Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. The outbreaks come as vaccination rates are slipping. More than 90% of people who have contracted the highly contagious virus were either unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination status. In Wisconsin, all but one of this year's cases were unvaccinated, according to the state measles dashboard. Wisconsin's last outbreak occurred in 2025 when the state recorded 36 measles cases in Oconto County. Local health officials managed to contain that outbreak before it ballooned out of control. Other states have endured months-long outbreaks with cases climbing into the hundreds. An ongoing outbreak in Utah that has infected 715 people first began in June 2025. In Wisconsin, local health officials are interviewing people with confirmed cases to identify close contacts. They are reaching out directly to those who may have been exposed to ask about their vaccination status and symptoms, Westergaard said. "We are hoping that this is a small outbreak, and that we contain it quickly," he said. "Right now, it’s a dynamic situation." Measles symptoms typically start a week to two weeks after infection with a high fever, cough, runny nose and red, watery eyes. A few days after the first symptoms arise, red spots or bumps appear on the face and near the hairline. That rash will spread to the rest of the body. People may also get tiny white spots inside the mouth a few days after symptoms begin. Immunization rates falling amid vaccine skepticism The latest cases in Wisconsin come as children prepare to head back to school this month and in September. Wisconsin kindergartners have one of the lowest vaccination rates in the country against measles, and the percentage of children getting the shot has fallen over the last decade, amid increasing skepticism of routine childhood vaccinations. Only about four of every five toddlers in Wisconsin have received the first dose of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine, which is highly effective at preventing illness, according to state data. Most of Wisconsin's latest cases are among children under 18 years old, according to the state Department of Health Services. And nationally, children or teens make up two-thirds of all measles infections this year, according to the CDC. Under President Donald Trump's administration, federal health officials have pushed to upend the nation's long-established childhood vaccine schedule. On Aug. 10, Trump signed an executive order calling for children to receive fewer vaccines and suggesting splitting some vaccines − including the one for measles, mumps and rubella − into multiple shots. The executive order is only a recommendation because states − not the federal government − set vaccination requirements for schools, childcare centers and other settings. Still, the American Academy of Pediatrics called the move "unscientific and dangerous," especially as measles cases reach a 35-year high. Top health officials in Wisconsin also took aim at the executive order, saying it contradicted the best available science on vaccines and disease prevention. "There is no new scientific evidence that justifies changing recommendations that have, and continue to, protect children across the United States," state Department of Health Services Secretary-designee Kirsten Johnson said in a statement issued Aug. 13. This story has been updated to add new information. Reporter Madeline Heim, of the Milwaukee Journal Sentinel, contributed to this report. Could an antiviral limit measles spread? Georgia State researchers say yes - Georgia Public Broadcasting

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Could an antiviral limit measles spread? Georgia State researchers say yes - Georgia Public Broadcasting

News Brief
Researchers at Georgia State University have reported preliminary pre-clinical success for an oral antiviral designed to limit measles-like disease spread, tested on ferrets using canine distemper virus as a model. The team, led by microbiologist Richard Plemper, found the antiviral could prevent disease in exposed contacts, though it is not intended as a substitute for the measles vaccine. With U.S. measles cases hitting 2,465 in 2026—the highest in two decades—the antiviral is positioned as a potential supplementary option for vaccine-hesitant populations. Human clinical trials have not yet begun, and the antiviral’s real-world impact remains unproven. The Pan American Health Organization will review the U.S. measles elimination status later this year.
Original source text (English)

Georgia State researchers say yes August 11, 2026 2:23 PM By: Sofi Gratas Share this page Share this page on Twitter Share this page on Facebook Share this page on Email Share this page on Print republish Primary Content play pause Listen Listening... 00:00 / 01:02 LISTEN: While not a replacement for the measles vaccine, an oral antiviral developed at Georgia State University could be an option for more vaccine-hesitant populations. GPB's Sofi Gratas explains. Caption False claims about vaccines such as the measles/mumps/rubella vaccine (above) have led to growing public mistrust in the disease-preventing measure. Credit: Adobe Stock The data is clear: Far fewer people, specifically children, are getting vaccinated against infectious diseases now than in the past. False claims about vaccines have led to growing public mistrust. And when these claims are amplified by public health officials, there are measurable results. Researchers out of Georgia State University are considering additional ways to prevent disease spread, by testing out an antiviral against measles-like diseases. To effectively test a potential antiviral treatment for measles, there would need to be clinical trials on humans. Microbiologist Richard Plemper and his team at Georgia State have instead tested on a cousin of the measles virus: canine distemper. The research, published in the science magazine Nature Microbiology, is funded by the National Institutes of Health. “We want to be able to have a drug that people can take at home — make the hurdle as low as possible,” Plemper said. They tested the oral antiviral pre- and post-exposure to the virus on ferrets. The key was to look at not whether the animals were exposed to the virus, which is inevitable, but if their immune systems recognized the pathogen and responded. In the pre-clinical trials, Plemper’s team found little to no detectable antibodies in the animals' immune systems. "We can completely prevent any disease in the contacts," Plemper said. "So complete success." If an antiviral were developed for human measles, it would not be a replacement for the measles vaccine, which is the most effective way to prevent disease. But Plemper says it could be a good option for people who are vaccine-hesitant, especially as the U.S. is on track to lose its measles elimination status. "Where we are now, we feel there is a need to augment vaccination, but with a pharmacological option," Plemper said. Uptake would still ultimately be up to the public. This fall, the Pan American Health Organization will review epidemiological evidence on measles outbreaks in the U.S. and decide whether the country has retained or lost its elimination status, held since 2000. There were more cases of measles this year — case counts currently stand at 2,465 — than in any other year in the past two decades. Georgia has largely been spared, with only five reported measles cases this year so far. That’s still double last year’s case count. 记录美国麻疹 暴发:田纳西州家庭在开学前应该了解什么

Record U.S. measles outbreak: What Tennessee families should know before school starts
新闻简报美国将在 2026 年经历创纪录的 麻疹 麻疹,截至年中报告确诊病例 2,465 例,为 35 年来的最高水平。近一半的病例涉及学龄儿童(5-19 岁),这在 2026-27 学年开始时引起了人们的担忧。田纳西州截至 2026 年已记录了 3 起 麻疹 案件,高于 2025 年的 8 起,不过该州幼儿园的 麻疹 发生率 (94%) 仍高于全国平均水平。卫生当局强调两剂 MMR 疫苗 剂量对于保护的重要性,因为 麻疹 很容易通过空气中的飞沫和表面传播,而没有可用的 抗病毒药物 治疗。田纳西州卫生部敦促对可疑接触者进行隔离和医疗咨询,特别是对于未接种疫苗的个人,同时指出存在宗教豁免,但低于全国平均水平。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Record U.S. measles outbreak: What Tennessee families should know before school starts

News Brief
The United States is experiencing a record measles outbreak in 2026, with 2,465 confirmed cases reported by mid-year—the highest in 35 years. Nearly half of cases involve school-age children (5–19 years), raising concerns as the 2026–27 academic year begins. Tennessee has recorded three measles cases so far in 2026, up from eight in 2025, though the state’s kindergarten vaccination rate (94%) remains above the national average. Health authorities emphasize the importance of two MMR vaccine doses for protection, as measles spreads easily through airborne droplets and surfaces, with no antiviral treatment available. Tennessee’s Department of Health urges isolation and medical consultation for suspected exposures, particularly among unvaccinated individuals, while noting that religious exemptions exist but are lower than the national average.
Original source text (English)
Find us on Google 📌 America's birthday 🎂 Start the day smarter ☀️ Get the USA TODAY app U.S. Politics Sports Entertainment Life Money Travel Opinion Crossword ONLY AT USA TODAY: Newsletters For Subscribers Real Estate From the Archives USA TODAY PLAY Crossword Marvel Comics eNewspaper Magazines Investigations Video Podcasts Humankind Advertise Your Business WITNESS (True Crime) Just Curious Best-selling Booklist Top Workplaces Nomination Legals Press Releases Cars Grocery Shopping OUR PORTFOLIO: Real Estate 10BEST USA TODAY Store Shopping Blueprint WITNESS (True Crime) Southern Kitchen Home Internet Services HEALTH Robert F. Kennedy Jr. Add Topic Record U.S. measles outbreak: What Tennessee families should know before school starts Afton Pratt Knoxville News Sentinel Aug. 10, 2026, 3:06 p.m. ET Hear this story A little over halfway through the year, the number of confirmed measles cases has reached a record high. As of Aug. 6, there were 2,465 cases, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. The number of cases has surpassed 2025 totals and is the highest recorded in 35 years. Children under 5 years old are among the age groups most at risk of contracting measles. With school starting for the 2026-27 academic year, including pre-K and kindergarten, there are steps parents can take to reduce their children's risk of contracting the virus. The disease was declared eliminated in 2000, but as vaccination rates decline, measles cases are on the rise, according to Reuters. U.S. Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. recently said he believes children should be vaccinated against measles despite his vaccine skepticism. Measles outbreaks have been reported in nearby Virginia and other Eastern states, including Pennsylvania. Nearly half of all U.S. cases have been recorded among people ages 5 to 19. With the back-to-school season on the horizon, here's what Tennessee residents should know about measles. What are the measles? The measles is a highly contagious virus that spreads through the coughing and sneezing of an infected person. Measles can live in the air for up to two hours after a person who has the virus leaves the area and anyone unprotected who comes into contact with the air or surface is at risk, according to the CDC. In order to be fully protected against the measles, the CDC says a person needs two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccines. Children can get the first dose between 12 and 15 months old. The second dose can be administered between ages four and six, according to the CDC. Measles symptoms usually begin seven to 14 days after exposure. Here's what to look out for: A high fever that can spike to over 104°F. A cough and runny nose. Red and watery eyes, or pink eye. Red and blotchy rash. There is no antiviral treatment for the measles. It is recommended those who contract the disease and are not protected get the vaccine up to three days after contact with the measles or receive an antibody shot than can help ease the infection and lower the risk of complications, according to the Mayo Clinic. Drinking water and resting are also recommended to help with the measles. Measles cases in Tennessee Although there is a rise in measles cases nationwide, Tennessee has seen only a few measles cases so far in 2026. As of Aug. 7, there have only been three measles cases this year, two were in Middle Tennessee and the third case was recorded in Shelby County, according to the Tennessee Department of Health. In 2025 there were eight confirmed measles cases in the state. With the return to school, parents might feel anxiety surrounding the decline in the measles, mumps and rubella vaccination rates, however the vaccination rate in Tennessee is above the national average, according to Johns Hopkins University. The most recent data shows that 94% of kindergartners received measles immunizations in the 2024-25 school year, the target is 95% and the national average is 93%. Tennessee was still down in vaccination rates compared to previous years. Tennessee allows religious exemptions for vaccinations, non-medical exemptions are sometimes linked to increased disease transmission, according to Johns Hopkins. Tennessee's non-medical vaccine exemption rate is lower than the national average. The TDH recommends isolating at home and calling your healthcare provider if you have suspicions that you have been exposed to the measles, especially if you are unvaccinated. Afton Pratt covers trending news for The Knoxville News Sentinel and Tennessee. She can be reached at ampratt@usatodayco.com. Featured Weekly Ad

Scale, impact and risk

Measles outbreak in Southwest Wisconsin grows; most taken ill weren’t vaccinated

威斯康星州卫生服务部 (DHS) 于 2026 年 8 月 14 日报告的 麻疹 暴发 确诊病例和疑似病例;国土安全部官员确认了当地活跃的 传播,并警告可能存在未被发现的病例;事件正在进行中并正在调查中。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles outbreak in Southwest Wisconsin grows; most taken ill weren’t vaccinated

News Brief
A measles outbreak in Southwest Wisconsin has grown to 23 cases, including 13 confirmed and six probable, with most patients unvaccinated, state health officials reported on August 14, 2026. The outbreak, centered in Iowa and Lafayette counties, marks the first locally acquired transmission in Wisconsin this year, with no travel links. One person has been hospitalized, and officials warn measles poses risks of severe complications such as pneumonia and brain damage. The Wisconsin Department of Health Services is coordinating with local agencies to trace contacts and isolate cases, while urging residents to verify their MMR vaccination status. Health authorities attribute the outbreak to declining vaccination rates below the 95% threshold needed for community protection and condemned a recent federal order to split the MMR vaccine as unsupported by science.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content HOME NEWS Politics & Government Work & the Economy Education Election 2026 Environment Civil Rights Commentary ABOUT SUBSCRIBE DONATE Part of States Newsroom Search Search Politics & Government Work & the Economy Education Election 2026 Environment Civil Rights 3:30 News Story Health Care Measles outbreak in Southwest Wisconsin grows; most taken ill weren’t vaccinated By Erik Gunn - August 14, 2026 3:30 am Wisconsin's top doctor says a new outbreak shows measles is spreading in the state. (Photo by Getty Images Plus) A report earlier this week of a couple of measles cases in Southwest Wisconsin has turned into an outbreak, with 13 more confirmed cases and six probable cases of the viral illness, the state health department reported Thursday. The outbreak, in Iowa and Lafayette counties, highlights the importance of getting vaccinated for measles, considered the safest and most effective way to prevent the spread of the virus, said Dr. Ryan Westergaard, the chief medical officer in the communicable disease bureau of the Wisconsin Department of Health Services. Ryan Westergaard, M.D., Wisconsin Dept. of Health Services The new group of measles patients who have been diagnosed are in Iowa County, Westergaard told reporters at a DHS briefing on the findings. Most of them were not vaccinated for measles, he said. DHS announced two measles cases Tuesday, one in Iowa County and one in Lafayette County, that were confirmed through tests at the Wisconsin State Lab of Hygiene. Those were the third and fourth measles cases in Wisconsin in 2026, and were not related to the first two this year, which were linked to out-of-state travel. Subsequent investigation by the local public health departments in the two counties turned up the 13 confirmed and six suspected cases that DHS announced Thursday. None of the current cases have been tied to travel. “The rapid emergence of these cases, particularly among people who have not traveled outside of Wisconsin, indicates that there is active local transmission, and it’s very likely that additional cases exist in the community that have not yet been identified or reported to public health,” Westergaard said. State tracks 23 cases so far Westergaard said DHS would not release the ages or age range for any of the people with confirmed measles cases in the current outbreak in order to protect their privacy. The department maintains a state registry for all measles cases that includes age ranges and vaccination status. Thursday evening, that record showed that of the 23 confirmed or suspected cases identified in Wisconsin in 2026, three were in children age 4 or younger, 11 were in children ages 5 to 17, and nine were in adults ages 18 or older. Westergaard said that it’s likely that there are more people with measles whose cases haven’t been identified and therefore aren’t on the state’s dashboard. With one exception, the patients identified in 2026 were not vaccinated for the measles virus, according to DHS. One has been hospitalized. Vaccine is ‘single best tool’ to stop outbreaks DHS is asking people to verify whether they’ve had the MMR vaccine — the shot that includes protection against measles, mumps and rubella. That information is probably available from a person’s regular health provider, if they have one, or from the Wisconsin immunization registry. The MMR vaccine is usually given in a two-shot sequence: the first dose when a child is 12 to 15 months old, and the second when the child is 4 to 6 years old. “Having received two doses of MMR vaccine provides exceptional, long-lasting protection, and this vaccine remains our single best tool to stop this and future outbreaks,” Westergaard said. For an adult without a record of the shot or who is uncertain about having had it, it is safe to get an additional shot, he said. DHS is also asking people to be familiar with measles symptoms. Westergaard said those include a high fever, a red rash that starts at the head and spreads down and covers the whole body, a cough, a runny nose and red or bloodshot eyes. A person who develops those symptoms, or whose family member experiences them, should call their regular healthcare provider, Westergaard said. People with the symptoms should call ahead before visiting a clinic, urgent care or the emergency room, he said, so that medical personnel can take precautions to prevent airborne spread of the virus before a patient arrives to protect other patients in the facility’s waiting room. Easily spread virus “Measles is known to be one of the most contagious viruses,” Westergaard said, “and we treat every case very seriously, because our goal is to completely stop measles transmission in the community anytime we have a case or a cluster of cases.” Measles can cause serious health problems, according to DHS — pneumonia, deafness, and brain damage as a result of swelling from the disease. Local public health agencies in the two counties haven’t identified specific public places where the virus might have spread, Westergaard said. One of the jobs of local public health departments is to track contagious illnesses such as measles in their communities or counties, he said. DHS supports local agencies as they work to trace who else has been exposed to the virus. Trump urges shift in childhood vaccine recommendations, calls for splitting up MMR vaccine When a person who’s been exposed has been identified, they’re monitored for symptoms “to make sure that if any new cases pop up, we can get them isolated right away,” Westergaard said. While the current outbreak is still small, “it’s a dynamic situation,” he added. With help from DHS, local health departments are tracing the spread of the virus and watching for more people who develop symptoms, “because that would be a sign that it’s getting larger.” New Trump order ‘contradicts the best available science’ Thursday’s announcement follows an order Monday from President Donald Trump. Wisconsin’s top health official said Thursday that Trump’s order goes against the best scientific recommendations for measles prevention. Trump’s order calls for separating the MMR vaccine into separate shots for each illness. “There is no new scientific evidence that justifies changing recommendations that have, and continue to, protect children across the United States,” said Kirsten Johnson, secretary-designee of Wisconsin DHS, in a statement released Thursday. “The executive order does not square at all with our recommendations on how to prevent measles or other vaccine-preventable diseases,” Westergaard said. “It was not science-based. It actually contradicts the best available science about how to prevent disease and keep kids healthy.” DHS early this year turned from the Trump administration’s Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s vaccine recommendations after the agency cast aside previous longstanding vaccine guidance. DHS instead has endorsed the childhood vaccine recommendations from the American Academy of Pediatrics and the American Academy of Family Physicians. Westergaard said vaccinations in Wisconsin have fallen below the 95% rate that helps ensure widespread protection from illnesses that vaccines can prevent, and are even below 90%. That has happened for many complex reasons, he said — including misinformation, sometimes spread deliberately. Wisconsin has so far avoided the “massive outbreaks” of measles that have occurred in states such as Utah, Texas and North Carolina, Westergaard said. Public health officials can contain the illness if they learn early of cases and are able to move quickly to identify and quarantine patients. But that’s more challenging because the state’s vaccination rate is lower than it should be, he said. In addition to encouraging more vaccination, cooperation with public health measures is “a second layer of defense,” Westergaard said. “Let’s work together as a community, as a team, when an outbreak happens — be willing to share information, be willing to stay home when any symptoms happen, and allow us to identify the extent of an outbreak and to stop it.”

B routine priorityPublic-health news

US CDC records more than 2,500 measles cases so far in 2026 - AOL.com

News Brief
The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported 2,566 confirmed measles cases in 2026 as of August 14, marking the highest annual total since the 1989–1991 resurgence. Nearly 93% of cases occurred in unvaccinated individuals or those with unknown vaccination status, while 169 hospitalizations were recorded with no deaths. Infections were documented in 45 states plus New York City and Washington, D.C., with 38 new outbreaks, including a large cluster in South Carolina that sickened nearly 1,000 people. The surge aligns with a sustained decline in MMR vaccination rates since the COVID-19 pandemic began.
Original source text (English)
US CDC records more than 2,500 measles cases so far in 2026 Updated Fri, August 14, 2026 at 2:56 PM UTC Aug 14 (Reuters) - The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention said on Friday there were 2,566 confirmed measles cases in the country so far this year, expanding the highest annual total in more than 35 years. Here are some details: • The overall 2026 figures represent the highest number of U.S. cases in a single year since a major resurgence of the highly contagious disease in the period from 1989 to 1991, with more than 55,000 infections and 123 deaths. • Measles-related hospitalizations this year stand at 169 so far with zero deaths, compared to 243 hospitalizations and three deaths in 2025. • As many as 93% of this year's cases were in unvaccinated individuals or people whose vaccination status was unknown, with the rest having received one or two doses of vaccine before being infected, the CDC said. • Infections were documented by the CDC this year in 45 states along with New York City and the District of Columbia. There have been 38 new outbreaks reported in 2026, compared to 48 in 2025. • A South Carolina outbreak had sickened nearly 1,000 people over six months after it began in October 2025 to become the largest single-location U.S. outbreak since measles was declared eliminated in the United States in 2000. • A surge in measles cases during the past two years has coincided with a steady slump in MMR immunization rates for children since the start of the COVID-19 pandemic. (Reporting by Christy Santhosh in Bengaluru; Editing by Anil D'Silva)

纽约男子因俄罗斯犯罪团伙洗钱医疗保险欺诈收益而被判刑;社区肿瘤学实践与全国癌症存活率相当或超过;科学家们发现了第一个可以治疗 麻疹 的人类抗体 – 晨间医学更新 - Medicaleconomics.com

New York man sentenced for laundering Medicare fraud proceeds for Russian crime ring; community oncology practices match or beat national cancer survival rates; scientists identify first human antibodies that could treat measles – Morning Medical Update - medicaleconomics.com

New York man sentenced for laundering Medicare fraud proceeds for Russian crime ring; community oncology practices match or beat national cancer survival rates; scientists identify first human antibodies that could treat measles – Morning Medical Update - medicaleconomics.com

News Brief
U.S. researchers have mapped the first comprehensive set of human antibodies against the measles virus, identifying a candidate antibody (4F09) that suppressed viral levels to undetectable in infected animals by locking the F protein and blocking cell entry. The NIH-funded team, led by Erica Ollmann Saphire at the La Jolla Institute for Immunology, used cryo-electron microscopy to resolve atomic-level interactions, challenging the prior assumption that protection relies solely on H-protein antibodies. Published in Cell Host & Microbe, the work points toward a potential therapeutic for individuals unable to receive vaccination amid rising global measles cases, though human trials have not yet been initiated.

Reynolds Listen 0:00 / 4:34 Key Takeaways Federal prosecutors tied a 37‐month prison term and $1,457,898 forfeiture to laundering Medicare fraud proceeds for a Russia-linked criminal network. Fraud operations leveraged nominee-owned DME companies and falsified corporate documentation to route Medicare reimbursements through U.S. accounts into shells and foreign banks. Community oncology care showed adjusted median overall survival of 46 vs 29 months in metastatic breast cancer and 12 vs 6 months in metastatic NSCLC versus SEER. Higher treatment initiation rates at independent practices likely contributed materially to observed survival advantages, including across subtypes with recent therapeutic innovation. Cryo-EM epitope mapping revealed protective anti-measles immunity extends beyond H protein, with F-targeting 4F09 suppressing viremia to undetectable levels in infected rats. Advertisement Morning Medical Update © batuhan toker - stock.adobe.com New York man sentenced for laundering Medicare fraud proceeds for Russian criminal organization Elnar Zarbailov was arrested in 2024 as he attempted to flee to Azerbaijan. A Staten Island man has been sentenced to 37 months in prison for laundering nearly $1.5 million in Medicare fraud proceeds on behalf of a transnational criminal organization based in Russia, as part of what the Department of Justice (DOJ) has called the largest health care fraud case it has ever prosecuted. Related content: Unmasking $14.6B in health care fraud: Inside the DOJ's largest-ever health care takedown Elnar Zarbailov, 42, a dual U.S.-Azerbaijani citizen, served as a fixer and money launderer for the organization, which used fraudulent durable medical equipment (DME) companies, fronted by nominee owners armed with false corporate documents, to deposit Medicare reimbursement checks into U.S. bank accounts and funnel the money to shell companies and overseas banks. Zarbailov deposited fraud proceeds from five DME companies and transferred funds to accounts including those located abroad. He was arrested at JFK Airport in September 2024 as he attempted to board a flight to Azerbaijan and pleaded guilty to conspiracy to commit money laundering in October 2025. In addition to his prison sentence, he was ordered to forfeit $1,457,898. Community oncology practices match or beat national survival benchmarks for two major cancers A new study found better or comparable overall survival for metastatic breast cancer and lung cancer patients treated at independent community oncology practices. Patients with metastatic breast cancer and metastatic non-small cell lung cancer treated at community oncology practices had overall survival rates that were consistently better than or comparable to national population benchmarks, according to a new study from the Community Oncology Alliance (COA) analyzed using the Flatiron Health Research Database (FHRD). After adjusting for age, sex, race and cancer subtype, median overall survival for metastatic breast cancer patients at community practices was 46 months, compared with 29 months in the national Surveillance, Epidemiology and End Results (SEER) database; for metastatic non-small cell lung cancer, the figures were 12 months versus 6 months. Researchers found that higher rates of treatment initiation at community practices likely drove a significant portion of the survival advantage, particularly for breast cancer subtypes where major therapeutic advances have occurred in recent years. The study, funded by the Community Oncology Alliance with support from BioNTech, Gilead, Johnson & Johnson and Pfizer, adds to a growing body of evidence that independent community practices deliver high-quality care to a patient population that includes historically underserved groups. Scientists map first comprehensive set of human antibodies against measles virus One antibody reduced measles virus levels in infected animals to undetectable levels, pointing toward a potential treatment for those who cannot be vaccinated. National Institutes of Health (NIH)-funded researchers have isolated and mapped the first comprehensive group of human antibodies targeting the measles virus, laying the groundwork for a potential measles treatment at a time when cases are rising in the U.S. and globally, according to a study published in Cell Host & Microbe. The team, led by Erica Ollmann Saphire, Ph.D., MBA, of the La Jolla Institute for Immunology, engineered more than 100 individual human monoclonal antibodies from a vaccinated donor and used cryo-electron microscopy to produce the first atomic-resolution maps of human antibodies bound to measles virus proteins. The findings upend a long-held assumption that protection against measles is driven almost entirely by antibodies targeting the virus's H protein; the study found that antibodies against the F protein can also confer powerful, independent protection. One F protein antibody, designated 4F09, was the most protective in the study, reducing measles virus levels in infected rats to undetectable levels by locking the protein in place and preventing the virus from entering human cells. No approved measles treatment currently exists in the U.S., leaving immunocompromised patients, pregnant women, and infants too young to be vaccinated without options. More than 470,000 measles cases were reported globally in 2024, and the U.S. has seen at least 72 outbreaks since January 2025. As measles cases continue to surge, are Pittsburgh schools prepared? - USA Today

B routine priorityPublic-health news

As measles cases continue to surge, are Pittsburgh schools prepared? - USA Today

News Brief
The article from USA Today, published on August 12, 2026, discusses the surge in measles cases in Pennsylvania, particularly in Pittsburgh's Allegheny County. As of August 10, 2026, there have been 232 reported cases, with 38 hospitalizations, all among unvaccinated individuals. Vaccination rates for kindergarteners in the county have declined over the years, with only 93.8% fully vaccinated in the 2025-2026 school year. Health officials are urging parents to ensure their children are vaccinated before the school year starts. The CDC notes that two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97% protection against measles, which is an airborne disease. The article also mentions President Trump's executive order on the administration of the MMR vaccine, though it is unclear if this directly impacts the current situation. The focus is on the importance of vaccination in preventing outbreaks, especially in schools where low vaccination rates increase the risk of transmission.

Please reference surfaced articles to validate AI summary Explore DeeperDive Ask USA TODAY anything President Trump recently issued an executive order regarding the administration of the MMR vaccine. Vaccination rates for kindergarteners in Allegheny County have steadily declined over the past several years. Health officials urge parents to vaccinate their children as measles cases climb in Pennsylvania. President Donald Trump just issued an executive order on how the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine should be administered to children. Measles, which was eliminated from the United States in 2000, has seen a resurgence as vaccination rates have declined. And as parents prep to send their kids back to school, cases are climbing in Pennsylvania, according to state data. While Allegheny County has no reported cases of measles, health officials are urging parents to vaccinate their children against the disease before they head back to the classroom later this month. Here's what to know about vaccination rates in schools throughout the county. Pittsburgh area school vaccination rates During the 2025-2026 school year, 93.8% of all students in Allegheny County were fully vaccinated, according to a report from the county's health department. Among the unvaccinated, 0.4% had medical exemptions, 2.2% had religious exemptions and 1.6% had moral/philosophical exemptions, while 1.4% were noncompliant but still attending school. Trending beyond this story Hurricane Forecast Kennedy Center Luigi Mangione NBA Schedule The percentage of kids entering kindergarten fully vaccinated was lower than the overall rate, with only 91.8% of students in that age range coming to school with all required vaccines. That population did not exceed 95% for any required vaccine except hepatitis B. Here's a breakdown of the percentage of kindergarteners who were fully vaccinated: MMR: 93.8% Varicella-Zoster Virus: 92.9% Hepatitis B Virus: 95.1% Polio: 93.5% DTaP: 93.7% Private/independent and public schools had higher rates of vaccinations among kindergarteners (above 93% for all vaccines), while religious schools saw the lowest rates of vaccinations, with no rates above 90% for all vaccines. Overall, 60.5% of schools had a student population that was at least 95% fully vaccinated, with public schools reporting the highest percentage of these schools at 69.6%, followed by private/independent schools, then charter schools. Religious schools had the lowest percentage at just 31.4%. Have student vaccination rates declined in Pittsburgh? When more than 95% of a community is vaccinated against measles, that community has achieved herd immunity, meaning most people are protected against the disease, according to the Centers for Disease Control. Kindergarteners in Allegheny County have been below that threshold for several years, according to state records. Here's how overall vaccination rates have dropped among kindergarteners in Allegheny County: 2025-2026: 93.8% 2024-2025: 94.1% 2023-2024: 94.4% 2022-2023: 94.8% 2021-2022: 95.7% 2020-2021: 96.7% How many cases of measles have there been in Pennsylvania, US this year? There have been 232 reported cases of measles in Pennsylvania as of Aug. 10, when the most recent update to Pennsylvania Department of Health's dashboard happened. Thirty-eight people have been hospitalized. All cases were among people who were not fully vaccinated. Nationwide, there were 2,465 confirmed cases of measles, according to the CDC. The agency noted that it was aware of probable measles cases but that unconfirmed cases were not included in its data. Ninety-three percent of those cases occurred in people who were unvaccinated or who had an unknown vaccination status. Meanwhile, 3% of people with measles had received one MMR dose, while 4% were fully vaccinated. Nearly half of cases (1,189 or 48%) were among people age 5 to 19. How well does measles vaccine protect against infection, hospitalization, death? Two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97% protection against the virus, according to the CDC, with children usually receiving their first dose at 12 to 15 months and a second at four to six years. It's estimated that one in five people who aren't vaccinated and get measles will need to be hospitalized, according to the CDC. Health experts have emphasized that the best protection against the disease is the vaccine, which can be given by itself, as part of an MMR vaccine or as part of the measles-mumps-rubella-varicella (MMRV) shot. How does measles spread? Measles is an airborne disease, which means that it spreads when an infected person exhales, coughs or sneezes, according to the World Health Organization. It can remain in the air for two hours after the infected person has left the area, and droplets can remain infectious on surfaces. A person with measles is contagious four days before the development of a rash to four days after the rash erupts, according to the WHO. What are the symptoms of measles? Signs of a measles infection generally appear seven to 14 days after someone encounters the virus. The following symptoms may be observed, according to the CDC: High fever Cough Runny nose Red, watery eyes Koplik spots (white spots inside the mouth) Rash that generally appears three to five days after the first symptoms, beginning as flat red spots on the face and hairline before spreading downward on the body Some people develop complications from measles. Complications may include: Ear infection Diarrhea Hospitalization Pneumonia Encephalitis, which can lead to convulsions, deafness or intellectual disability Subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, a fatal disease of the nervous system Death Pregnancy complications, which may include premature birth or a low-birth-weight baby A Billionaire's Reading List: 5 Books Cuban Says Everyone Should KnowCuban reads for three hours every morning. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Confirmed measles case in Knox County - Knox Pages

News Brief
A confirmed measles case was identified in Knox County, Ohio, on August 3, 2026. The individual has completed their isolation period. The Ohio Department of Health and Knox Public Health are collaborating to trace potential exposures and encourage vaccination. Contacts of the individual are being notified and advised on quarantining measures. Measles is a highly contagious virus that can spread before symptoms appear and may lead to severe complications, including hospitalization, encephalitis, and death. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recommends two doses of the MMR vaccine for children, starting at 12-15 months and 4-6 years of age. Unvaccinated individuals are urged to consult their healthcare providers for vaccination options. For further information, visit Knox Public Health’s website.
Original source text (English)
MOUNT VERNON — A laboratory-confirmed case of measles was identified on Aug. 3 in a resident residing in Knox County. The case has completed its isolation period. The Ohio Department of Health is working with Knox Public Health (KPH) to follow up on potential exposures and promote opportunities for vaccination. Knox Public Health is also working with the individual and their healthcare provider to identify contacts. KPH will notify the contacts and provide guidance on quarantining. Measles is a highly contagious virus. Individuals infected with measles can spread it to others, even before they have symptoms. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in the air after an infected person leaves the room. Symptoms usually appear 7 to 14 days after a person comes in contact with the virus, but it can take as long as 21 days for symptoms to appear. Measles symptoms include high fever (may spike from 103° F to 105° F), cough, runny nose, pink/red, watery eyes, and a red/brown blotchy rash. The rash will appear on the face and then spread to the trunk and extremities. Rash and other symptoms usually subside in 7 to 9 days. According to ODH, about one out of every five people who get measles will be hospitalized. One out of every 1,000 people with measles will develop brain swelling due to infection (encephalitis) which may lead to brain damage. One to 3 out of 1,000 people with measles will die, even with the best care. Treatment recommendations The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recommends all children get two doses of measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine, starting with the first dose at 12 through 15 months of age and the second dose at 4 through 6 years of age. Anyone traveling internationally should be fully vaccinated before traveling. Infants 6-11 months old should get one dose of the MMR vaccine before travel, then should receive two additional doses after their first birthday. Older children, adolescents, and adults should receive one or two doses of MMR vaccine if they do not have evidence of immunity. If you have received two doses of the MMR vaccine, your chances of getting measles are very low. After two doses, the vaccine is 97% effective at preventing measles. Residents are encouraged to talk to their healthcare provider about vaccination if they are unvaccinated or do not know if they are vaccinated. The MMR vaccine is safe and effective with hundreds of millions of doses given. Contact your medical provider or Knox County Community Health Center at 740-399-8008 if you have questions about measles or the MMR vaccination or would like to schedule a vaccination appointment. If you have symptoms, stay home, avoid contact with others, and call your healthcare provider. Do not show up in the waiting room without contacting the staff first in case you are contagious. For more information and updates, visit Knox Public Health’s website

A confirmed measles case was identified in Knox County. The Ohio Department of Health and Knox Public Health are working to follow up on potential exposures and promote vaccination opportunities. Knox Public Health is also working with the individual and their healthcare provider to identify contacts.
Scale, impact and risk
The measles virus is highly contagious and can spread to others even before symptoms appear. It can live in the air for up to two hours. Symptoms typically appear 7 to 14 days after exposure, but may take up to 21 days. The risk of hospitalization is about 1 in 5, and the risk of encephalitis is 1 in 1,000, which may lead to brain damage. The mortality rate is 1 to 3 in 1,000, even with the best care.
Response, status and uncertainty

俄勒冈州的 麻疹 突破三年来新高 - KATU

Measles in Oregon surpasses three year high - KATU
新闻简报Oregon has reported 45 measles cases this week, surpassing the 2024 peak of 31 and reaching the highest level in decades. This surge coincides with a statewide decline in MMR vaccination rates, with only 88% of 2-year-olds vaccinated compared to the national average of 90% to 91%. The state's measles rate is one case per 100,000 people, higher than Washington and California. Public health officials are investigating the outbreak, citing reduced vaccination rates as a contributing factor. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) is tracking the national trend, but no specific containment measures have been detailed. The situation highlights concerns about herd immunity and the impact of declining immunization rates on public health.

WATCHREAD MORE Oregon measles cases hit highest level in decades as vaccination rates fall by KATU Staff Thu, August 13, 2026 at 3:14 PM Updated Thu, August 13, 2026 at 5:16 PM Play/Pause Video Qualityauto640x360, 828kbps854x480, 1488kbps1280x720, 2341kbps1920x1080, 3771kbps Speed0.25x0.5xnormal1.5x2x Audio Track Audio Qualityauto SubtitlesopenoffCaptions (CC1) Font sizedefault50%75%100%150%200%300%400% Font styledefaultitalicbold Font familydefaultmonospaced serifproportional serifmonospaced sans serifproportional sans serifcasualcursivesmall capital Font colordefaultwhiteblackredgreenbluecyanyellowmagenta Font opacitydefault100%75%50%25% Character edgedefaultraiseddepresseduniformdrop shadowed Character edge colordefaultwhiteblackredgreenbluecyanyellowmagenta Background colordefaultwhiteblackredgreenbluecyanyellowmagenta Background opacitydefault100%75%50%25%0% Window colordefaultwhiteblackredgreenbluecyanyellowmagenta Window opacitydefault100%75%50%25%0% BackReset 00:00 02:53 PlayMute Picture-in-PictureApple AirPlayGoogle CastVRSettingsFullscreen CG - Oregon Measles Record - 5p Replay logo 3VIEW ALL PHOTOS FILE: A child shows signs of a measles infection. 35Comment Share topics: Measles Oregon MMR Vaccination rates CDC Outbreak Epidemiologist Immunocompromised PORTLAND, Ore. (KATU) — Oregon reached 45 known measles cases this week as of Wednesday, surpassing 2024's peak of 31 cases. The state has not seen this many measles cases since a 1991 spike, when it recorded 100 cases. In 2025, the state had only one case. The increase comes amid a statewide decline in the rate of measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccinations. Currently, only 88% of Oregon's 2-year-olds have received an MMR vaccine. The national average is 90% to 91%. "We've seen several years of declining vaccination rates that really have been going on for about the last decade. As we went into the pandemic, we saw kind of a plateauing of that, but as we came out of the pandemic, we saw more parents and choosing not to fully vaccinate their children with the MMR, or measles, mumps, and rubella vaccine, as well as other vaccines," said Dean Sidelinger, epidemiologist for the state of Oregon. READ ALSO | West Coast Health Alliance sticks with pediatric vaccine schedules despite Trump order The spike in new measles cases is occurring across the nation. According to data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), there were 85 cases nationwide in 2000. So far this year, there have been 2,465 cases. Powered By 10 Sec Oregon measles cases hit highest level in decades as vaccination rates fall Next Stay Oregon has a higher per capita rate of measles cases than other Western states such as Washington and California. Oregon's rate has reached one case per 100,000 people, compared with 0.6 per 100,000 in Washington and 0.1 per 100,000 in California. Many other states with high case counts are Republican-leaning states. For example, Utah has recorded 524 cases this year, South Carolina has recorded 670 and Texas has recorded 190. According to the CDC, measles affects the respiratory system first before spreading throughout the body, causing high fever, cough, runny nose, red, watery eyes and a distinct rash. It can lead to severe illness or hospitalization, especially in young children and people with weakened immune systems. According to the National Foundation for Infectious Diseases: About 1 in 5 unvaccinated people in the U.S. who get measles will be hospitalized. The risk is higher in children younger than 5. One out of every 1,000 people with measles will develop brain swelling, which could lead to brain damage. As many as 1 in 20 children with measles will get pneumonia. One to three out of every 1,000 people with measles will die, even with the best care. According to the CDC, people who have received both doses of the MMR vaccine have only a 3% chance of contracting the disease if exposed. The MMR vaccine is less effective in people who are immunocompromised and children younger than 1, which is why health officials recommend the first MMR vaccine for children 12 months or older so it can be more effective. Measles can be much more dangerous for both groups if contracted. However, Sidelinger noted that because Oregon is currently experiencing a measles outbreak, parents of children 6 months or older can consult their pediatrician about giving their child the first MMR shot early to provide an extra layer of protection. If a child receives the first shot before turning 12 months old, a second shot is recommended at the 1-year mark for lifelong immunity. For parents of children who are too young to be vaccinated or who are immunocompromised, Sidelinger advises checking the school's vaccination rate and discussing how vaccinations are managed in a day care or school setting. JOIN THE CONVERSATION (35) He also suggested asking the school about its plans if a measles case is reported and whether staff members are required to receive MMR vaccines. by Taboolaby Taboola SPONSORED CONTENT A Billionaire's Reading List: 5 Books Cuban Says Everyone Should KnowBlinkist Magazine | SponsoredSponsored Undo Doctors Identify 10 Popular Medications Now Linked to High Blood Pressure (See the List)See Why | SponsoredSponsored Undo Cardiologists Name 7 Popular Foods Now Tied to High Blood Pressure (Full List)You tube | Not Just Age | SponsoredSponsored Undo MORE TO EXPLORE 23-year-old dance instructor accused of sexually abusing teenage students Undo Why you should avoid getting cash from a credit card at the ATM Undo Man who murdered 11-month-old stepdaughter returns to prison after early release Undo Recommended For You 6 dead, 1 critically injured in Michigan shooting What’s delaying the demolition of Portland’s Centennial Mills building? B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

随着 麻疹 暴发 的增长,俄亥俄州报告了 69 例 - Cleveland 19 News

Ohio reports 69 cases as measles outbreak grows - Cleveland 19 News
新闻简报截至2026年8月13日,俄亥俄州报告了69例麻疹确诊病例,较前一周确诊的45例大幅增加。暴发 始于 2026 年 7 月 23 日报告的两例病例,与 Sugarcreek 和 Bloomfield 的暴露事件有关。有五人需要住院治疗,该病毒具有高度传染性,通过咳嗽和打喷嚏传播。卫生官员强调了 疫苗接种 的重要性,并指出两剂 MMR 疫苗 的有效率达 97%。麻疹病毒 可以在空气中保持传染性长达两个小时,并发症在 5 岁以下儿童、20 岁以上成人、孕妇和免疫系统较弱的人群等弱势群体中更为常见。地方和州卫生部门正在进行接触者追踪并通知暴露者。该州将在 11 月份参加国际讨论,以确定其是否失去了 麻疹 的自由状态。
查看新闻五要素
时间
截至 2026 年 8 月 13 日星期四,确诊病例较上周确诊的 45 例呈上升趋势。暴发 至少自 2026 年 7 月 23 日报告前两例病例以来一直在进行。
地点与对象
美国俄亥俄州,病例分布在多个县,包括阿什塔比拉、凯霍加、富兰克林、吉奥加、霍姆斯、诺克斯、渥太华、特朗布尔、塔斯卡瓦斯、韦恩和未知。受影响人群的年龄范围从婴儿到40岁。
事件
根据俄亥俄州传染病仪表板,截至 2026 年 8 月 13 日,俄亥俄州报告有 69 例 麻疹 确诊病例。健康专家将最近的病例激增归因于 Sugarcreek 和 Bloomfield 的暴露事件。45 名感染者中有 5 人必须住院治疗。
规模、影响与风险
麻疹病毒 已超过 2025 年的病例总数,2026 年确诊病例为 69 例。麻疹病毒 传染性极强,通过咳嗽和打喷嚏传播,可在空气中保持传染性长达两个小时。并发症在 5 岁以下儿童、20 岁以上成人、孕妇和免疫功能低下的个体中更为常见。麻疹 疫苗(MMR)安全有效,两剂有效率达97%。
应对、状态与不确定性
地方和州卫生部门正在积极努力识别并通知可能接触过病毒的个人。健康专家建议,如果怀疑存在 麻疹,请联系医疗保健提供者进行检测,并避免前往医疗机构,无需事先通知。该州正在监控 麻疹 并将于 11 月参加国际讨论,以评估 麻疹 的无状态。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Ohio reports 69 cases as measles outbreak grows - Cleveland 19 News

News Brief
Ohio has reported 69 confirmed measles cases as of August 13, 2026, marking a significant increase from the 45 cases confirmed the previous week. The outbreak, which began with two cases reported on July 23, 2026, has been linked to exposure events in Sugarcreek and Bloomfield. Five individuals required hospitalization, and the virus is highly contagious, spreading through coughing and sneezing. Health officials emphasize the importance of vaccination, noting that two doses of the MMR vaccine are 97% effective. The measles virus can remain infectious in the air for up to two hours, and complications are more common in vulnerable groups such as children under 5, adults over 20, pregnant women, and those with weakened immune systems. Local and state health departments are conducting contact tracing and notifying exposed individuals. The state will participate in international discussions in November to determine if it has lost its measles-free status.

On GoogleAdd as a preferred source on Google OHIO (WOIO) - As of Thursday, the Ohio measles outbreak has grown to 69 confirmed cases so far this year, according to the state’s infectious disease dashboard. In July, the number of measles cases in the U.S. surpassed the tally for all of 2025. Ohio has now done the same, with cases trending up from the 45 confirmed last week. The state had 45 measles cases in 2025, seven cases in 2024 and one case in 2023. Here is the latest data for Ohio in 2026. County Name Number of Cases Ashtabula 17 Cuyahoga 3 Franklin 7 Geauga 16 Holmes 2 Knox 1 Ottawa 1 Trumbull 2 Tuscarawas 8 Wayne 2 Unknown 7 Those with measles have ranged in age from an infant to 40 years old. Five of the 45 infected individuals had to be hospitalized, according to the dashboard. As far as the recent surge, health experts are pointing to exposure events in Sugarcreek and Bloomfield. The Ashtabula County Health Department first reported two cases of measles back on July 23. Since then, local and state health departments continue working to identify and notify anyone who may have been exposed. People who have weakened immune systems may take longer to experience symptoms. According to experts, anyone seeking testing should contact their healthcare provider. You should not arrive at a healthcare facility without calling ahead if measles is suspected. Measles are preventable through vaccines and have been considered eliminated from the U.S. since 2000. In November, international health officials will meet to determine if the U.S. and Mexico have lost their measles-free status. Here are tips on how to spot symptoms of measles and how to protect yourself, from the Ohio Department of Health: Measles is extremely contagious and can spread to others through coughing and sneezing. It can be brought into Ohio by individuals who travel to places where measles is spreading. If people are not protected against the disease, nine out of 10 people who are exposed will become ill. The measles vaccine (called MMR as it protects against measles, mumps and rubella) is safe and effective at preventing measles. Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is extremely low. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in the air when an infected person coughs or sneezes. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before through four days after a rash appears. Measles symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite, and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts 5-6 days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications may also occur. Complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in youth. Copyright 2026 WOIO. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Kentucky health officials report confirmed measles case, suspect outbreak after additional unconfirmed case reports - WSMV

News Brief
Kentucky health officials confirmed a measles case in Christian County on Thursday, August 13, 2026, and suspect a wider outbreak due to multiple unconfirmed reports. The confirmed case involved an unvaccinated individual exposed by an out-of-state visitor from an area with an ongoing measles outbreak. Officials are investigating potential exposures, notifying at-risk individuals, and offering guidance. Measles is a highly contagious respiratory virus that can lead to serious health complications, particularly in young children. The outbreak is part of the largest measles outbreak in the U.S. since 2000, with cases confirmed in Tennessee, Alabama, Missouri, Georgia, and other states. The situation remains under investigation, and further details are expected as the health department continues its efforts.

Catch Up Tennessee Department of Health confirms state’s second case of measles this year in Middle TN FRANKFORT, Ky. (WSMV) - The Kentucky Department of Public Health announced Thursday that a case of measles with a high number of community exposers was confirmed in Christian County. Officials said the individual with the confirmed case was not vaccinated against the measles and is believed to have been exposed by an out-of-state visitor from an area with an ongoing outbreak. Additionally, multiple unconfirmed reports of measles in Christian County is leading Kentucky health officials to suspect a wider outbreak in the area. “Measles is a serious, debilitating and sometimes life-threatening disease,” said Kentucky Cabinet for Health and Family Services Secretary Dr. Steven Stack. “Fortunately, measles is almost entirely preventable for those who have been vaccinated with two doses of the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine. We urge all parents and caregivers to talk to their healthcare providers about their child’s immunizations and how best to keep their families healthy.” The Kentucky Department of Public Health is working to identify other potential exposures, notify those possibly at risk and provide guidance on next steps. Kentucky health officials said that measles is a “highly contagious respiratory virus that can cause serious health complications, especially in young children.” The virus spread through the air, where it can remain for up to two hours. Early symptoms of measles include high fever, cough, runny nose and red/watery eyes. Those symptoms can start eight to 12 days after exposure. Kentucky health officials said the measles rash normally appears about three to five days after symptoms begin, and it typically starts at the face before spreading down the body. “The U.S. is currently experiencing the largest measles outbreak in since it was declared eliminated in here in 2000,” Kentucky health officials said. “As of Aug. 6, the CDC had reported more than 2,465 confirmed cases of measles across the country, the highest number of cases in a single year in 35 years.” Cases of measles have been confirmed in Tennessee, Alabama, Missouri, Georgia and more, according to the CDC. 时间
麻疹 病例于 2026 年 8 月 11 日星期一确诊,患者最近刚从国际旅行中返回。该文章发表于2026年8月12日。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Santa Barbara County Reports First Measles Case Since 2019 - independent.com

News Brief
Santa Barbara County has reported its first measles case since 2019, confirmed on August 11, 2026, involving a person who recently returned from international travel. The case was identified by the county's public health department, which is now in infection control mode and working with healthcare providers to trace potential exposures. The patient's residence was not disclosed for privacy reasons. This isolated case aligns with a broader U.S. measles resurgence, with 2,465 cases reported in 2026. Public Health spokesperson Jessica Martinez stated the virus was likely passed by a visitor, emphasizing the high vaccination rate in the tri-county area, which provides herd immunity. The situation remains under investigation, with no further details on the source or spread of the virus.
Original source text (English)

Arts & Entertainment Santa Barbara Live Music Venues Living Food & Drink Opinion Events Sports Obituaries Real Estate Indy Parenting Cover Stories Wedding Santa Barbara Green Guide Classifieds Advertise About Us Contact Us Create Event Subscribe News Health Santa Barbara County Reports First Measles Case Since 2019 Public Health Confirms Person Who Recently Returned from International Travel Has Come Down with Virus By Jean Yamamura Tue Aug 11, 2026 | 5:55pm Share this: Share on Facebook (Opens in new window) Facebook Share on X (Opens in new window) X Email a link to a friend (Opens in new window) Email Print (Opens in new window) Print Add independent.com on Google Add the SB Independent as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google. Credit: Courtesy The measles has been steadily making its way toward Santa Barbara and arrived this week. County Public Health confirmed Monday that a person who’d recently returned from international travel came down with the virus. The department is in infection control mode and coordinating with healthcare providers over potential exposure. They could not state where in the county the patient resided for privacy reasons, said spokesperson Jessica Martinez. In the recent nationwide outbreak, Ventura County confirmed one case of measles earlier this month and another was reported in San Luis Obispo in December 2025. Santa Barbara had a transient finding of measles virus in its wastewater in September 2025, but it was just once. Public Health’s Martinez said the department believed it was passed by a visitor. “If it was a local case, you would anticipate multiple in a row,” she said. Measles began a resurgence in the U.S. in January 2025 when cases spread among a population of people in Texas with low vaccination levels. So far, 2,465 cases are reported in the U.S. in 2026, according to the Centers for Disease Control. In 2025, 2,289 cases were confirmed compared to 285 in 2024. The tri-county area is considered to have a high rate of vaccination, 95 percent or more, which brings a level of herd immunity. Measles generally starts with a fever and runny nose and eyes about two weeks after exposure, with a rash appearing about four days in. Vaccination is safe and effective, healthcare professionals attest, and lasts for a lifetime. Before this, Santa Barbara County’s last case of measles was in 2019. 克拉克县确诊新 麻疹 病例,使今年病例总数达到 17 例

New measles case confirmed in Clark County, bringing year’s total to 17
新闻简报华盛顿州克拉克县于 8 月 12 日确认了新的 麻疹 病例,使该县 2026 年病例总数达到 17 例。周五,这名儿童在访问 AFC 紧急护理卡马斯后检测呈阳性,同时具有传染性,暴露了未接种疫苗的患者和访客。公共卫生部门警告在诊所接触过的未接种疫苗的个人进行 21 天的自我隔离,并在寻求护理之前联系医疗服务提供者,以防止进一步传播。今年的 17 例病例中有 15 例涉及未接种疫苗的儿童,并发症最有可能发生在幼儿和老年人中。在全国范围内,麻疹病例已达到35年来的最高点,凸显了麻疹的紧迫性;MMR 疫苗 在两次注射后可提供高达 97% 的保护。
查看新闻五要素
时间
发表于2026-08-12;所提供的证据中未明确报告事件日期。克拉克县公共卫生部门于周二(默示发布日期)确认了这一新病例。
地点与对象
美国华盛顿州克拉克县;暴露组:周五下午 AFC 紧急护理卡马斯 (180 N.E. 192nd Ave.) 的患者和访客;受影响人群:未接种疫苗的儿童和成人、12个月以下的婴儿。
事件
克拉克县公共卫生部门报告了一例新确诊的 麻疹 病例。该病例涉及一名儿童,他在前往亚足联紧急护理卡马斯后检测呈阳性,同时具有传染性。麻疹 已确认。
规模、影响与风险
克拉克县年初至今总数:17 例 麻疹 病例(1 月 7 例、2 月 1 例、7 月 8 例)。17 例中有 15 例涉及未接种疫苗的儿童;疫苗接种 2 名成人病例状况不明。周五下午在诊所未接种疫苗的个人存在暴露风险。麻疹具有高度传染性;病毒通过空气传播并可能持续存在。症状包括发烧、咳嗽、流鼻涕、眼睛发红和皮疹。5 岁以下儿童和 20 岁以上成人的并发症风险较高。国家背景:CDC 报告,截至 8 月,已有 2,465 例 麻疹 确诊病例,创 35 年来新高。
应对、状态与不确定性
公共卫生部门敦促在诊所接触过的未接种疫苗的个人 21 天内待在家里并避免前往公共场所。暴露者应在就诊前致电医疗保健提供者,以避免暴露给其他人。公共卫生部门推荐疫苗接种;MMR 疫苗 一剂后的有效率约为 93%,两剂后的有效率约为 97%。居民可以通过州的 MyIR 移动系统检查 疫苗接种 记录。调查正在进行中;所提供的证据中没有关于接触者追踪或额外暴露的更多细节。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

New measles case confirmed in Clark County, bringing year’s total to 17

News Brief
Clark County, Washington, confirmed a new measles case on August 12, bringing the county’s 2026 total to 17 cases. The child tested positive after visiting AFC Urgent Care Camas on Friday while contagious, exposing unvaccinated patients and visitors. Public Health warns unvaccinated individuals exposed at the clinic to self-isolate for 21 days and contact providers before seeking care to prevent further spread. Fifteen of the 17 cases this year involved unvaccinated children, with complications most likely in young children and older adults. Nationally, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, underscoring the urgency of vaccination; the MMR vaccine provides up to 97% protection after two doses.
Original source text (English)
Clark County Public Health has confirmed a new case of measles in the county, the agency announced Tuesday. A child tested positive after being seen at AFC Urgent Care Camas at 180 N.E. 192nd Ave., while they were contagious. People who were at the clinic from 2 p.m. to 6:30 p.m. Friday may have been exposed. People who were at the clinic at that time, have not been vaccinated against measles or have not had measles in the past are at risk for getting sick and should stay home and out of public places for 21 days, Public Health said. During that time, anyone who believes they have symptoms of measles should call their healthcare provider before visiting the medical office to make a plan that avoids exposing others. So far this year, Clark County has had 17 cases of measles. Seven cases were reported in January , one in February and eight in July . Fifteen of the 17 cases involved unvaccinated children; the vaccination statuses of two adult cases were unknown. Measles is a highly contagious and potentially serious illness caused by a virus. The virus spreads through the air after a person with measles coughs or sneezes. A person with measles can spread the virus before they show symptoms, and the virus can linger in the air after someone who is infectious has left. Measles symptoms begin with a fever, cough, runny nose and red eyes, followed by a rash that usually starts on the head and spreads to the rest of the body. A person exposed to measles will begin to get sick about one to three weeks after exposure. People are contagious up to four days before and after the rash appears. Public Health urges people who are not protected against measles to get vaccinated. Residents who have questions about the vaccine or are unsure whether they are protected should talk to their healthcare provider. Vaccination records are also available through the state’s MyIR Mobile system. Measles poses the highest risk to people who have not been vaccinated, including infants younger than 12 months. The MMR (measles, mumps and rubella) vaccine provides the best protection against measles. One dose of the vaccine is about 93 percent effective at preventing measles. Two doses are about 97 percent effective. Measles can be serious for all age groups. However, children younger than 5 and adults older than 20 are more likely to suffer from measles complications. The U.S. has hit a 35-year high in measles cases with the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reporting 2,465 confirmed cases as of Aug. 6. Event
A new confirmed measles case was reported by Clark County Public Health. The case involved a child who tested positive after visiting AFC Urgent Care Camas while contagious. Measles is confirmed.
Scale, impact and risk
Year-to-date total in Clark County: 17 measles cases (7 in January, 1 in February, 8 in July). 15 of 17 cases involved unvaccinated children; vaccination status of 2 adult cases unknown. Exposure risk to unvaccinated individuals at the clinic on Friday afternoon. Measles is highly contagious; virus spreads via air and can linger. Symptoms include fever, cough, runny nose, red eyes, and rash. Complications risk higher in children under 5 and adults over 20. National context: CDC reports 2,465 confirmed measles cases as of August, a 35-year high.
Response, status and uncertainty
Public Health urges unvaccinated individuals exposed at the clinic to stay home and avoid public places for 21 days. Exposed individuals should call healthcare providers before visits to avoid exposing others. Public Health recommends vaccination; MMR vaccine is ~93% effective after one dose and ~97% after two doses. Residents can check vaccination records via state’s MyIR Mobile system. Investigation ongoing; no further details on contact tracing or additional exposures reported in the supplied evidence.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Donald Trump boasts that flu vaccine is no longer recommended for kids as measles cases soar - The Mirror US

News Brief
U.S. President Donald Trump announced a policy reducing the number of recommended childhood vaccines from 18 to 11, including influenza, hepatitis B, and COVID-19, while asserting that the MMR vaccine may be 'quite lethal' if administered in a single visit. The announcement coincided with a 35-year high in measles cases, with 2,318 confirmed cases reported in the first seven months of 2026 and two unvaccinated child deaths in 2025. Medical experts criticized the policy, warning it could lower vaccination rates and increase infection risks. The guidance, framed as non-binding, directs the attorney general to challenge state-level vaccine mandates, though school requirements remain under state jurisdiction. A federal panel is scheduled to decide in November 2026 whether the U.S. retains its measles eradication status amid rising vaccine hesitancy and declining immunization coverage.
Original source text (English)

The MMR vaccine might be "quite lethal" if administered all at once, according to the president. He likened it to injecting a child's body with a bottle of Pepsi. A single dose of MMR is 0.5 ml, according to the FDA. View 4 Images A panel is scheduled to determine in November whether the U.S. will still be considered to have eradicated measles(Image: AFP via Getty Images) In response to a reporter's request for proof of the "lethal" assertion, Trump stated, "What I've heard is that there are some people that say it is that way." According to recent data from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), the number of measles cases in the U.S. has reached a 35-year high. Just seven months into 2026, there have been 2,318 confirmed cases of measles in the nation, up from a high of 2,289 instances the previous year, when two unvaccinated children died from the illness. Click here to follow the Mirror US on Google News to stay up to date with all the latest news, sports, and entertainment stories View 4 Images More cases of measles have been reported in the U.S. in the last 18 months than in the previous 25 years(Image: AFP via Getty Images) The U.S. has seen a rise in vaccine hesitancy in recent years, resulting in lower immunization rates and more cases. Article continues below A panel is scheduled to determine in November whether the U.S. will still be considered to have eradicated measles, a status that Canada lost last year. After a year or more of sustained transmission of the same measles virus strain, a nation risks losing its eradication status. More cases of measles have been reported in the U.S. in the last 18 months than in the previous 25 years. ENJOYED THIS STORY? Utah has the nation’s highest measles rate, following years of declining vaccination - fox13now.com
新闻简报犹他州的 麻疹 率全国最高,该州和南卡罗来纳州报告的病例占全国总病例数的 48%。这篇发表于2026年8月11日的文章指出,犹他州的麻疹率已从2009-2010年的97.7%下降到2025年的88.6%,在美国排名第43位。这种下降导致麻疹病例大幅增加,每1000人中就有1人感染。暴发 地区感染。犹他州未接种疫苗的个体感染 麻疹 的几率为 1:440,而接种疫苗的个体感染 麻疹 的几率为 47,000 分之一。过去两年,有 617 名未接种疫苗和 68 名已接种疫苗的犹他州人确诊感染 麻疹。文章强调了 疫苗接种 率对于预防 暴发 和实现群体免疫的重要性,这需要 95% 的覆盖率。

犹他州的 麻疹 比率多年来一直在下降,而该州的 麻疹 比率是全国最高的。该文章发表于 2026-08-11,最后更新于同一天。

暴发 集中在三个卫生区:犹他州东部的三县卫生区、犹他州中部卫生区和西南区。这些地区占犹他州人口的 12%,但占该州 麻疹 病例的 64%。

犹他州是全国人均 麻疹 病例数最差的州,过去 15 年 麻疹 病例数显着下降。文章报道,该州的疫苗接种率已降至88.6%,在全国排名犹他州第43位。

今年到目前为止,全国范围内已有 2,465 起 麻疹 案件。犹他州和南卡罗来纳州合计占这些病例的 48%。在麻疹地区,每1000人中就有一人在这个麻疹期间感染了麻疹。在未接种疫苗的犹他州人中,大约每 440 人中就有 1 人感染过 麻疹,而对于接种疫苗的犹他州人来说,这一数字下降至大约每 47,000 人中就有 1 人。过去两年中,共有 617 名未接种疫苗的犹他州人感染了 麻疹,而 68 名接种疫苗的犹他州人已确诊患有该疾病。
应对、状态与不确定性
如果某人接种了一次或多次疫苗,该州就将其视为已接种疫苗,但指南建议接种两次。暴发 集中在三个卫生区,文章强调了 疫苗接种 发病率下降对 麻疹 病例的影响。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Utah has the nation’s highest measles rate, following years of declining vaccination - fox13now.com

News Brief
Utah has the nation's highest measles rate, with 48% of the country's total cases reported in the state and South Carolina. The article, published on August 11, 2026, notes that Utah's vaccination rate has dropped from 97.7% in 2009-2010 to 88.6% in 2025, ranking it 43rd in the U.S. This decline has led to a significant increase in measles cases, with one in every 1,000 people in outbreak districts infected. Unvaccinated individuals in Utah have a 1 in 440 chance of contracting measles, while vaccinated individuals have a 1 in 47,000 chance. Over the past two years, 617 unvaccinated and 68 vaccinated Utahns have been confirmed with measles. The article emphasizes the importance of vaccination rates in preventing outbreaks and achieving herd immunity, which requires 95% coverage.

Tweet Email Utah has the nation’s highest measles rate, following years of declining vaccination Prev Next By: Max Roth Posted 11:22 PM, Aug 11, 2026 and last updated 11:54 PM, Aug 11, 2026 SALT LAKE CITY — Utah is the worst state in the country for measles cases per capita, and the numbers behind that distinction reveal how dramatically the state's vaccination landscape has shifted over the past 15 years. Nationally, there have been 2,465 measles cases so far this year. Utah and South Carolina together account for 48% of those cases. On a per-capita basis, Utah is the nation's hottest hot spot for measles outbreaks. WATCH: Students mix measles outbreak with a return to Utah classrooms Students mix measles outbreak with a return to Utah classrooms The state's vaccination rate tells a significant part of the story. In 2009 and 2010, Utah had the seventh-highest kindergarten vaccination rate in the nation at 97.7%. Last year, that rate had fallen to 88.6%, ranking Utah 43rd in the country — the third-biggest decline in vaccination rates of any state over that period. That matters because measles requires 95% vaccination coverage to achieve herd immunity. Utah is now well below that threshold. The outbreak is concentrated in three health districts: the Tri County District of eastern Utah, the Central Utah Health District, and the Southwest District. Those districts represent 12% of Utah's population but account for 64% of the state's measles cases. In those rural outbreak districts, one in every 1,000 people has already caught measles during this outbreak. On the Wasatch Front, that figure is one in 13,000. Measles by the numbers in Utah: Measles by the numbers in Utah Vaccination status is a defining factor in individual risk. Of unvaccinated Utahns, about one in every 440 has caught measles. For vaccinated Utahns, that figure drops to approximately one in every 47,000. In total, 617 unvaccinated Utahns have gotten measles in the last two years while 68 vaccinated Utahns have been confirmed with the disease. In 30 cases, vaccination status is unknown. The state counts someone as vaccinated if they've gotten one or more shots, though guidelines recommend getting two.
